- **Processing (outbox jobs on the `llm` lane):** `submit_learning` returns the item's ID at once, having queued a `kb_route` job in the same transaction; routing an item queues its `kb_draft`, and so does anything that clears or retargets its draft (an admin's retarget, a stale draft sent back at approval, a reopen). The outbox keeps one unclaimed job per item and kind; a step made due while a job of its kind is claimed (running, or backing off) gets a job of its own, so a change the running step committed before can't be lost. A claim (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`) holds an `llm` job for a 5-minute lease (above two model calls' deadlines), so a second runner never takes a step mid-call; a thrown failure backs off 30 s, 1 and 2 minutes. Each job re-reads the item and runs its step only while it is still the item's next (`kbStepOf`: route without a target, draft with a target and no draft; a document proposal is its own draft); otherwise it is dropped. A failure (an LLM error or an unusable answer) spends one of the job's three attempts and then fails it; an unavailable or busy model, or an item that changed under the step (decided, merged into, retargeted), asks to run again later without spending one (an item that changed runs again at once). While the KB models are down only one job a minute calls them; the rest wait. An item's pipeline state on the Knowledge page (`processing`, its error, attempts and next try) is read from its step's newest job, never stored on the item; an admin's Retry queues a fresh job beside the failed one. The `llm` runner starts with the `llm` job switch, or `kb`; on start it queues the due step of any open learning whose step has no job waiting (self-healing; a failed one is left for an admin's Retry). The migration that moved the queue (0048, which also locks the old table until the move in 0049 commits) gave every open item waiting for a step a job: items that had failed got a failed job, except those that failed only because Bedrock was busy, which start again with no attempts spent, and a claimed or backing-off item was made due at once (every step is idempotent).
  1. *After submission (Opus, two calls):* route the item (step 2), then deduplicate it by LLM comparison (no embeddings yet) against open items, approved items (statement and target), rejected items (statement and reason) and the current text of the routed target's section (the whole target if it has no section, truncated when long); each list is capped at the newest 100. The dedupe prompt doesn't lead: the model first restates the learning's specific fact in one sentence, then classes each of the few closest candidates (at most 5) as *same fact*, *related topic only*, *unrelated* or *contradicts*; only *same fact* may close or flag the item as a repeat or as covered, and only *contradicts* may flag a contradiction. The default answer is new: overlapping topic, tool or area is not coverage, and when unsure it answers new, since an admin reviews every open item. The restated fact and the classifications aren't stored, but every claim needs them: a claim on a candidate the model classed otherwise, or didn't class at all, is dropped (relations are compared ignoring case, `_` and `-`). Outcomes, written in one transaction with conditional writes: a near-duplicate of an open item is closed as **merged** into it, which gains the evidence, its globs and its occurrence count; a match to a rejected item is closed as **suppressed** (re-read in the same transaction: it must still be rejected, and not kept apart from the item, as a merge's or coverage's item must still be open or approved and not kept apart; it is written back unchanged on the version read, so a change committed meanwhile rolls the outcome back to be retried); something an approved item already says is closed as **covered**, and that item gains the evidence and count (evidence fields only, never its decision); coverage by the target's text only flags: the item stays open (and is drafted as usual) with "May already be covered by <target › section>", the quoted sentence and the model's one-line reason, and the admin approves, rejects or keeps it; every claim must quote the text the model was shown (an item's statement, or the target's text), checked mechanically (whitespace, case, and sentence punctuation or quote marks at either end ignored; the quote is kept without those ends, and they never count towards its length), and a claim whose quote isn't found is dropped, leaving the item open; a merge or suppression claim needs two quotes, one from the open or rejected item's statement and one from the new item's own, each found in its own statement (a quote found only in the other statement is dropped), and the closed item keeps both quotes (`by: intake`), which its card shows ("Merged on intake into <ID>" or "Suppressed: matches rejected <ID>", then "“…” states the same fact as “…”"); every closing claim (merge, suppression, or coverage by an approved item) also needs each quote it rests on to be at least 4 words and 20 characters (whitespace collapsed, as consolidation's), else the item stays open, flagged with the other item's quote and a note that it was too short to close the item on: "May already be covered by approved item <ID>", "May repeat open item <ID>" or "May match rejected item <ID>" (the last two with the item's own quote too), naming which quote was too short, and only one such flag at a time (suppression's, then merge's, then coverage's, then the target's), though a target flag hidden this way is shown beside it ("The current text may say it too", with its quote and reason); a flag never names an item an admin kept apart from the item (a target flag it hid is shown instead); when a merge closes the item, its flag for a rejected item moves to the open item it merged into, unless that has a flag already or is kept apart from the rejected item, naming the merged item its own quote is from ("merged-in <ID>"); a target with no text yet (a new document, an empty overlay) can't cover or contradict anything; a contradiction with the target's text or an open or approved item keeps the item open with the contradiction flagged. Merged, suppressed and covered items leave the open queue, keep their links and can't be decided; an admin can reopen one that has a target (back to the open queue with it, drafted again but not routed or deduplicated again; the item it was closed against keeps any evidence it gained). Each LLM call has a deadline (2 minutes; two calls stay below the `llm` lane's 5-minute claim lease) so a stalled call can't hold the pipeline; unusable answers, LLM errors and timeouts get up to three attempts in all (the job's), with the lane's backoff between them (the same for drafting); the item is then marked routing-failed, stays open and can still be decided by hand, or an admin retries it (back to routing without a target, to drafting with one). An *LLM unavailable* failure (expired AWS sign-in, no credentials, no model access) is not an attempt: its job asks to run again a minute later with its attempts given back, and its card says "Waiting: AI unavailable — <reason>" (no Retry; it resumes by itself), so a credential lapse never fails items. A *Bedrock busy* answer (ThrottlingException, ServiceUnavailableException ("Bedrock is unable to process your request"), ModelNotReadyException, ServiceQuotaExceededException, InternalServerException, SDK timeouts) waits the same way without an attempt, but with exponential backoff and up to 25% jitter (1, 2, 4, 8, then 15 minutes, counted in memory per item, so a restart starts over at a minute) and says "Waiting: Bedrock busy (retrying at HH:MM UTC)"; findings, review splits and search jobs wait the same way, and only real failures (unusable answers) spend attempts. Items that had gone `failed` with a busy message before the queue moved were carried over as pending jobs with no attempts spent (0048). While either KB model is down (health is tracked per model, so intake's model doesn't count) only one job a minute calls them (`kbExecutors`): that call is the probe that tells when the LLM works again, and the other jobs wait for the rest of the minute without spending an attempt, their cards saying they wait for the AI ("Waiting: AI unavailable — the KB models are down…"). A credential or access failure that surfaces only when a call's deadline fires still waits rather than counting as an attempt. Document proposals (`/kb-bootstrap`) skip routing and drafting: their target is the proposed document, and the proposal is its own draft.
  2. *Routing:* a project fact goes to a document for the relevant audience (e.g. "generated files live in `src/gen/`"), unless it is a short rule that always applies to one agent, which goes in that agent's definition (e.g. "never edit `src/gen/`"). Process behaviour goes to the agent's definition (e.g. "write the regression test before the fix"). The target is a document or agent file and the heading it belongs under; an agent-file target always means the board's overlay (its `## Board rules`, or the whole file for one the board owns), so the section is a heading there or none to append. When no document fits, the target is a new document with a suggested name, area, audience and description. The submitter's suggested target is a hint. Anything that would hold for any project is also flagged as a catalog candidate with a one-line reason, shown to admins as a suggested PR to slop's `catalog/`.
  3. *Drafting (Opus, one call per routed open item):* every routed item is drafted, not only recurring ones. Opus gets the statement, type, evidence (including near-duplicates'), source globs, the target and the target's full current text: a document's body; for an agent file the board's overlay, with the catalog text as read-only context (only the overlay can change); for a new document its proposed name, area, audience and description and the board's document index, to avoid overlap. It answers with one section's full new text (the existing heading it replaces, which may be a better heading in the same target than routing chose, or none to add a new section), or a new document's whole body, plus a one-line rationale. Slop splices the section into the target (up to the next heading of the same or a higher level) and shows the diff of the whole text, and records the target version the draft was made against. Failures retry with backoff like routing, then mark the item drafting-failed (still decidable). A draft whose target has since changed (another approval or edit) goes back to be drafted again: every write to a document or agent-set file re-queues the open drafts against it in the same transaction, a target that changes during the Opus call releases the item, and approving a stale draft is refused and re-queues it.
  4. *Weekly consolidation (Opus, the routing model):* a `board_jobs` job, `consolidation`, run per board when 7 days have passed since its last run (checked hourly, after mining when both are due so the stale rule reads that week's below-threshold counts; items mining raises are still pending, so they are compared once drafted, the next week; under a lease and lease-conditional result like mining's, its lease covering the worst case: 21 calls to the LLM deadline, 15 minutes for the writes, and a mining run's 30-minute lease, since its merges and stale pass wait on mining's board lock), with Run now on the Knowledge page beside mining's status. It merges open items that state the same fact under intake dedupe's safeguard, and flags stale ones; a model never closes an item on its own say-so.
     - *Candidates:* open statements the pipeline has settled (drafted, or failed), not document proposals, newest 200. Items still being routed or drafted are the pipeline's.
     - *Candidate pairs:* one call gets each candidate's ID, type, target, evidence count and statement (one JSON object per line, so a statement can't run into the next), and proposes at most 20 pairs that may state the same fact (none when unsure). Proposing closes nothing. Pairs an admin kept apart are dropped. Pairs remembered as not the same fact (Memory) are listed after the candidates as already checked; any proposed anyway are counted and dropped before the cap of 20, so they don't crowd out new ones.
     - *Verification:* one call per pair restates A's fact and classes B as same fact, related topic only, unrelated or contradicts; for same fact it quotes each statement. A pair merges only as same fact with both quotes found verbatim (ignoring case and whitespace) in their statements, each at least 4 words and 20 characters (whitespace collapsed); anything else, an unusable answer or a failed call leaves both open.
     - *Memory:* the job remembers (`board_jobs.state`) the pairs it verified as not the same fact, by each item's ID and a hash of what the calls read of it (statement, type and target), and doesn't verify them again until either item's statement, type or target changes (an admin's edit or target change); new evidence, mining's weekly refresh, a stale flag or a merge into the item keep it. It keeps the newest 500 such pairs. When a run leaves no pair unanswered (every pair merged, found different, or refused by the rules; a pair whose item an earlier pair merged away is verified against the survivor in the same run, unless that pair was already asked), and the candidates it leaves are the ones it compared (none added, none gone but its merges, none's statement, type or target changed; another writer meanwhile, such as an item drafted or edited, means they aren't), it also remembers the candidates as it left them, by the same hash; the next run makes no candidate-pair call while they are unchanged.
     - *Merge:* the survivor is a drafted item before one the pipeline gave up on, then the more repeated item, then the older. It keeps its draft and target and gains the other's evidence, globs and count (as an intake merge), and its signal when it has none; the board's `kb_signals` rows for the closed item point at the survivor. The other is closed as merged into it, with both quotes (its card says "Merged by weekly consolidation" with them), and any stale flag on it cleared. Both writes are conditional on the versions verified, in one transaction that also takes mining's board lock; a pair whose item changed meanwhile is skipped.
     - *Keep apart:* reopening a merged item records the pair on both items in the same transaction, so consolidation never merges them again. A merge carries both items' kept-apart sets onto the survivor, and a pair is refused when either side holds the evidence of an item kept apart from the other, so no chain of model-verified merges (in one run or across runs, by consolidation or intake dedupe) joins what an admin split.
     - *Stale (a documented rule, no model):* a settled open item is flagged stale when it has no evidence newer than 60 days (its submission, evidence merged into it, its signal's last measurement), or when it is mined and its signal has been below its threshold for 4 runs in a row. The flag only shows on the card with Not stale (admins) beside Reject; nothing is closed. New evidence clears it, and Not stale clears it and suppresses it for 60 days.
     - *Order:* the open queue is ordered by evidence count (occurrences, or source globs if more), then the freshest evidence; cards show "evidence from N globs".
     - While the routing model is down the job is skipped without calls (the last run stands) and the next hourly check tries again, even after a Run now within the week; a run that finds it down mid-way keeps the merges it made. A failed run (of either job) keeps the last run too, and is tried again after 1 hour, then 2, 4 and so on, doubling with each failure in a row up to 24 hours; a skip in between keeps the count, so a model that alternates between down and failing still backs off. Each run records the candidates, pairs proposed and verified, merges, pairs skipped, pairs already checked, whether the candidates were unchanged, and items flagged stale or cleared; the Knowledge page shows an unchanged run as such, and a failed run with when it tries again.
- **Merge policy:** which paths clash between globs is one board knowledge row of its own kind (`merge_policy`, name `merge-policy`), built like the local-run spec: JSON `{ "exclusivePaths"?: string[], "sizeIgnoredPaths"?: string[], "rolloutPaths"?: string[], "subGateCheck"?: string }`, validated by hand in core on every write and read (an object with no other keys; each list at most 50 path globs relative to the repo root, each non-empty, one line, at most 200 characters, no leading `/` and no `..`, repeats dropped; `{}` is valid: the policy exists and nothing is configured), stored in canonical form. It changes only through an approved KB item (a learning about which paths clash, or which are left out when a sub is sized, is routed or retargeted to it and drafted as the whole value; the first value arrives the same way; there is no import, upload or edit door), and an agent may not approve it (a person decides which globs hold for each other). It is not part of the agent set: no agent-set version bump, not in the bundle, effect checks go by time. A stored value that fails the check is never served or acted on (the Knowledge page shows the problem and the policy acts as empty). The Knowledge page shows it read-only. `exclusivePaths` are path globs that only one open glob may change at a time (for slop, `apps/server/drizzle/**`, since migration numbers and the journal clash). `sizeIgnoredPaths` are left out when a sub's size is measured: defined here and validated only; the sub gate reads it in a later change. `rolloutPaths` are path globs whose merge needs a person after the image deploy (default when none: `infra/**`, `apps/server/src/config.ts`, `.github/workflows/**`, `**/drizzle/**`); see Rollout steps below. `subGateCheck` is the name of the check run a sub waits for on its PR head before slop applies the size and sensitive-path policy and merges it: one non-empty line of at most 100 characters, default `checks` when absent (no migration: a board whose repo still reports a `sub-gate` run sets `"subGateCheck": "sub-gate"` through a KB item before updating). The board's `sensitivePaths` stay a board setting for now; moving them into the merge policy is a later change.
- **Exclusive paths (holds):** when a glob is about to start a routine run (created as a sub or with `autoTrigger`, changed to a sub, Start on a same, or released: row 33), slop checks whether another open glob (one with a branch, not merged) may change an exclusive path: its branch already does (the code host's compare against the base, read through the `BranchFiles` port and cached for a minute per branch head; GitHub lists at most 300 files; a branch that can't be read counts as unknown and never holds), or it is in Doing with no file there yet and a plan that names one. It can't know beforehand whether the new glob will touch those paths, so it applies the hold only when the new glob's plan.md names an exclusive path (the literal directory of an exclusive glob) or a migration (the words "migration", "schema", "drizzle"), or intake's file guess includes one; otherwise the glob starts and is warned (row 35) once its branch touches an exclusive path another open glob also changes. A hit becomes an **overlap** (`impliedAfter`: `{ id, paths }`, shown as "overlaps s15b18"), which since s15f44 never holds the start: the glob starts at once and the later merge rebases; the patterns it is predicted from carry learned weights (see Edge kinds). A hit that would make a cycle is skipped. Intake returns the file guess (`files`) and `suggestedAfter` (unmerged globs the request says to wait for after "after", "once", "until" or "wait for"); both are advisory.
- **Review:** the KB view shows each open proposal (most evidence first, then the freshest evidence) with its target as a path, the diff (computed against the target's current text, with context; long diffs collapsed), rationale, evidence with links to the source globs (`/boards/{b}?glob=<id>` opens a glob's view, or the signed-off page's), occurrence count and the number of globs its evidence comes from, flags (stale, with Not stale; catalog candidate with its reason, possibly covered by the target's text, or by an approved item on a quote too short to close it, with the quote, contradictions with links, stale draft) and processing state (routing, drafting, the last error and next try while retrying, waiting for the AI with its reason and no Retry, failed with Retry); members see it read-only. Merged, suppressed and covered items (with Reopen for admins), and approved and rejected ones with their outcome or reason, are listed collapsed: the newest 50 of each group (decided items by when they were decided) with their totals, and older ones on request, up to 1,000. A draft section is never a document's level-1 title when it has lower-level headings (that would replace the whole document); such a draft is appended instead. For now only board admins approve (one click applies the draft: the section is spliced into the current text, documents keep their frontmatter, a new document gets its proposed one, agent files write only the overlay), edit then approve, change the target (the draft is cleared and redone against it, without routing again), keep the statement as an approved learning (served by `get_conventions`), or reject with a reason (this may be opened up later). An admin's agent can also clear the queue through MCP (`list_kb_items`, `decide_kb_item`): the same service calls as the buttons (version, drafted-against version, validation, history, agent-set version bump and effect-check baseline), recorded as the signed-in admin with `via: 'agent'` inside the item's `outcome` (a rejection by an agent records `outcome: { kind: 'rejected', via: 'agent' }`; no column of its own), and the Knowledge page shows "via agent" beside the decider. An agent approves the item's draft as drafted, or with `editedDraft`, which goes through the page's edit-then-approve path (validation, drafted-against version check, splice, history, agent-set version bump). Approving is always refused (use the Knowledge page) for the local-run spec, the merge policy (slop executes or enforces them) and whole-document proposals. Agent-set targets (agent, command, hook, settings, mcp, claude_md) and items with contradicts flags are refused too unless the board's `agentKbApproval` setting is `docs_and_agent_files` (default `docs`; an admin sets it in Settings, Automation, under Knowledge, "Agents may approve agent-file and contradicting items"; stored as the `boards.agent_kb_approval` column); a contradicting item then needs a `reason` (why the contradiction is intended or resolved), recorded as the decision's reason. Rejecting any item is allowed. The Knowledge page's "Decided by agent (last 7 days)" filter lists the agent's decisions, and an admin can Reopen any of them: an approval's change is reverted through a new knowledge version (refused when the target changed since, or the approval created the document) and the item returns to the open queue to be drafted again. Approval creates a new document version in Postgres (and a new agent-set version for agent items), picked up by the next `sstor init` or routine run. Rejections are kept to suppress repeats.
- **Effect check:** after an approved change, slop watches the signal that triggered it (e.g. the rate of "missing test" findings per glob) over the following globs that ran with the change. If it hasn't dropped after a per-board number of globs, or another signal got markedly worse, slop raises a proposal to revise or revert the change, with the before and after figures. Humans still decide.
  - *Which items:* approved items with a signal: mined items, and a submitted item when the admin picks one of the board's signals as measured now ("Watch signal" when approving; none by default). A watch signal is refused for an item that already has a signal (its check watches that one) and for a document proposal. `dependency:` signals aren't measured by effect checks (the check reads no manifests, and documenting the dependency is the fix), so they aren't offered under Watch signal and approving a dependency item starts no check. Approving sets the check watching, and the approval checks that item on its own at once in the background so the before figures show straight away (not a run of the job, so the board's daily schedule stands); a failure there is logged and never fails the approval. A signal picked this way belongs to the approved item in `kb_signals` (unless an open item already holds it), claimed under mining's board lock, so mining doesn't raise it again while the check owns it. Approving a revise-or-revert item watches the signal its original watched; so does approving the open item a revise-or-revert item was merged into by consolidation, when it had no signal of its own and took the `effect:` signal over.
  - *Attribution:* an agent-file change is measured by agent-set version: its outcome records the first agent-set version with the change (the board's version after the approval), and a glob counts after the change when its version is at least that one. An approval that left the file as it was bumps no version, so it is measured by time (the content was already being served). A glob's version is its commits' `Slop-Agent-Set` trailer, else the highest version on its artifacts, failures or learnings; globs with no known version are left out. Documents and approved learnings are served at runtime without a version, so they are measured by time: a glob counts when its first recorded work (its first artifact, run trigger or pick-up) came after the approval. An approval recorded before agent-set versions were kept in outcomes is measured by time too.
  - *Windows:* N globs a side, from the board setting `effect_check_globs` (default 10; admins set 3 to 50 in Settings, Automation; a watching check follows the setting, a final one keeps the N it was decided on). Only merged globs count, so the card's figures can differ from the mined item's, which count every eligible glob in mining's 4-week window. Before: the last N globs first merged before the approval that are eligible for the signal (the signal's own per-glob rule, e.g. globs with builds for "CI failing after local checks passed"); a glob that merges again later (Merge and continue) stays on this side, and this side counts only activity up to the approval, so work done with the change never moves the before figures. After: the first N eligible globs, by first merge, that first merged after the approval and ran with the change. Each side's rate is its affected globs over its globs. Every run reads the activity from a year before the approval.
  - *Verdict*, once the after side has N globs: *improved* when the rate after is at most 70% of the rate before (so also when it fell to none), otherwise *not improved*; *worse elsewhere* when it improved but another signal is markedly worse over the same globs: at least 3 globs eligible for that signal on each side, its rate after at least twice its rate before and at least 20 points higher. *Unmeasurable* when no glob before the approval is eligible. The comparisons are exact (whole numbers), so a boundary isn't lost to rounding.
  - *Raised item:* for not improved or worse elsewhere, a mined KB item of the original's type. Not improved: "Revise or revert s15kN (<statement, cut>): after N globs with <agent set vN or later | work started after <date>> the rate of <label> is X% (a/b), against Y% (c/d) before." (naming the worst other signal that rose too, if any). Worse elsewhere leads with the gain to keep, then the side effect over that signal's own globs: "Revise s15kN (<statement, cut>): after N globs with <basis> the rate of <label> fell from Y% (c/d) to X% (a/b), but the rate of <other label> rose from … to …." Its evidence gives both sides with their globs; its suggested target is the original's target, its signal key `effect:<item>`. It goes through the KB pipeline like any mined item, except that dedupe never sees the original or another item raised for it, so it can't be closed as covered by the change it questions. The check records it and is final; the card's link follows the item to the one it was merged into.
  - *Schedule:* a daily `board_jobs` job, `effect_check` (checked hourly with the other jobs, under its lease), with Run now and its last run on the Knowledge page. Each item's check runs in its own transaction under the board's effect-check lock and re-reads the item, and its write is conditional on the item's version, so two runs never raise twice. No LLM calls.
  - *Shown on the approved card:* "Effect check: watching (3/10 globs since v12)" (or since the approval date) or the verdict, with the basis and when it was last checked, a before/after table (affected, of, rate, the affected globs as links; the after row is "so far" while watching; rows for any signal that got worse), and a link to the raised item.
- **Data:** a KB item is its decision glob (no table of its own; see Decision globs and the knowledge page): the glob holds the statement, status (open, approved, rejected, merged, suppressed, covered, read from the decision's outcome and `closedAs`), type, target (kind, name, section, or a proposed new document), source submitted or mined, the triggering signal for mined items (key, kind, agent, label, window, figures, globs, examples), catalog candidate and reason, occurrence count, the stale flag with its reason and when an admin last kept the item, and its links to other items (duplicate, suppression, coverage, possible coverage, contradiction, kept apart); its artifacts hold the payload (statement and evidence), the drafted section with the target version it was drafted against and its rationale, the approval's outcome and an approved item's effect check (`effect_check`: state, the signal key and label watched, basis, N, before and after figures with their globs, signals that got worse, the raised item, when last checked); its attachments hold the evidence near-duplicates added and the quotes a consolidation merge or an intake merge or suppression closed it on; its processing state is its pipeline jobs in the outbox; later an embedding. These sit alongside versioned knowledge documents and agent items; a `review_sources` table (the queued local review artifact with its text, commit, agent-set version, location and split state); and a `review_findings` table of classified findings per glob and commit (source, commit, agent-set version, severity (in scope, suggestion, unknown), round, path and line, text, fingerprint, class and the classifier's note, classification state). A glob's sources and findings are deleted with it. `kb_signals` holds one row per board and signal key (the item it last raised, its last figures and measurement, when it was raised, and the runs in a row below its threshold); `board_jobs` holds each board job's last run, its result, the lease of the server running it, and what the job keeps between runs (`state`: consolidation's checked pairs and candidates, and the sub limit's remembered bug references and failed asks). `boards.effect_check_globs` holds the effect check's N; `boards.sub_max_changed_lines` holds the learned sub size limit and `sub_limit_changes` its history (Sub review); an approved agent-file outcome records the board's agent-set version after it (`agentSetVersion`).
- **Cost:** the KB pipeline is the most important part of the system, so it uses Opus 5.5 (Bedrock's global inference profile at the base price, with default sampling): two calls per submission (routing, dedupe) plus one draft per routed item (and per re-draft when its target changes), cents per learning. Weekly consolidation costs at most one Opus call over up to 200 statements plus up to 20 verification calls per board per week, and none while the candidates are unchanged; pairs already found different aren't verified again, and failed runs back off to one try a day. Review findings cost one Haiku split call per free-form local review, then one Haiku classification call each. The learned sub limit costs one Haiku call per new bug report that names a recently merged sub (remembered unless it blames it, at most 3 tries without a usable answer), and none while the model is down.

Approved changes are served on the next fetch; no PR is needed.

## Review, merge and GitHub

From now on the team works with a branch and PR per glob, replacing direct commits to master. Slop links everything in GitHub to globs by one convention: the branch name is the glob ID, and slop creates that branch itself.

**Rollout steps and capabilities (as built, s15f112).** *Parse:* when an implementation record (kind `implementation_plan`, or the `postplan` alias) is stored, its `## Rollout` section becomes the glob's rollout work steps (kind `rollout`, due `after_merge`, blocking sign-off and dependants, for anyone), origin `rollout-section`. Items are list items (`-`, `*`, `+` or numbered) at the section's top indent, `script:`, `setting:` or `manual:` (no prefix counts as manual), with an optional parenthesised suffix of comma-separated `key: value` pairs in any order, `(env: <environment>, check: <capability>, blocks: sign_off)`; deeper bullets are detail, and a script's first fenced block is its body, stored as a `rollout_script` artifact (label `rollout: <title slug>`, a new version only when the body changes). `None`, `n/a` or an empty section has no steps. A check that is not a known capability, or any other unknown key, is dropped and said so in the detail. Titles are plain text: a bold kind (`**manual:** x`) reads as the kind, and emphasis or a code span around the whole title is dropped (s15b138). A `script: <repo path>` line under a step points it at a committed script (its `script` path, which wins over a fenced body) and isn't shown; the same inside a longer detail line (a path with a `/` or a script extension) counts too, and that line stays in the detail. **On the dev environment or the base:** no `env` means after the merge (the stage named after the base branch, due `after_merge`, blocking sign-off and dependants); `env: <dev environment>` makes the step due `after_deploy:<name>`, blocking nothing unless `blocks` (any of `sign_off`, `reviewing`, `dependants`) says so. An after-deploy step is due once the glob's branch is deployed to that dev environment, which can be before the merge; the moment is stored on the step and sticky, so a later deploy or rollback doesn't un-due it. **Downstream (s15t262):** a `downstream` option on a step, `(downstream)` or `(downstream: int, staging, prod)` (and `downstream` on `add_human_step`), makes slop add one non-blocking step "Run on <names or the rest of the pipeline> as each is released: <title>" (see Environments and deployment); names come from the board's environments knowledge doc, and a person ticks it. An after-merge step never looks due while the glob is in Merging. Steps are matched by title on a re-read, or by the stored title as the parser writes it now (so `**Do x**` stored before plain titles is `Do x`): open ones that changed are updated, open ones that are gone are dismissed, done and dismissed ones keep their state (only a changed title is saved), and the same record twice changes nothing. Real steps dismiss the investigator's placeholder (origin `investigation`) and an open `rollout-paths` step. *Path-only flag:* a final merge (not a Merge and continue) queues `check_rollout_paths` with the merge commit; its executor reads the commit's files and, if any match the board's `rolloutPaths` and the glob has no step from the record and its latest record has no non-empty Rollout, adds one `rollout` step "Rollout steps not written: check what this change needs" (origin `rollout-paths`), once. A diff that cannot be read flags nothing. *Capabilities:* `/api/health` carries `capabilities` (booleans: `fileAttachments` when the bucket is configured and answers a HEAD request, cached a minute; `attachmentEvents`, `slack`, `codeIndex`, `deployResults` from their configuration), shown in the System group of the board's Settings page (collapsed, read-only). The `capabilities` job (every minute) ticks every open step whose `check` capability is on and whose due-ness has arrived (a step due now; an after-merge step once the glob is merged and no longer in Merging; an after-deploy step once its dev environment's branch deploy has succeeded): done, `doneBy: slop`, event `HumanStepChanged` with `checked: true`; the glob then moves on as for any completed step.

**Once steps and board environments (as built, s15t237).** A step takes effect either by deploying it (the pipeline) or by doing it (a person runs it against the live thing: IAM, account or GitHub settings). `HumanStepDue` gains `once` beside `now`, `after_merge` and `after_deploy:<env>`; it needs no migration (`due` is text). *Once:* `add_human_step` / REST take `due: 'once'` (MCP also `once: true`; any other `due` beside `once: true` is refused), and a record's `## Rollout` item marks it `(once)`, `(once: true)` or `(once, check: …)` (`RolloutStep.once`; an `env` beside it is dropped with a note in the detail; `rolloutWhen` gives due `once` and the after-merge blocks, `sign_off` and `dependants`). `stateOf` makes a once step `due` when it has no repo script, its script is an artifact, the glob is merged, the branch wasn't checked, or the script's path is in `HumanStepFacts.scriptsOnBranch`; otherwise `pending`. `GlobService.get` fills `scriptsOnBranch` from `onceScriptsOnBranch` (`CodeView.read(board, <glob id>, path)` for each open once step's path while the glob has a PR and isn't merged; a code host that can't answer leaves it unset, so a person is never held up by a check slop could not make). Other readers (the board list, whose-move) don't check the branch, so there a once step is due. A once step never reopens: `failRolloutStep` leaves a done once step done, and refuses a once step as the failed step (`failHumanStep` routes it to the "is there a fix glob?" suggestion instead of starting a generation). `openRolloutSteps` counts it, so a sub that gains one becomes a same, as for any step that holds back sign-off. *Environments:* `addHumanStep` refuses `after_deploy:<env>` when `<env>` isn't one of the board's dev environments, with the board's names in the message (`unknownEnvironmentText`), so MCP and REST both refuse; `reconcileRollout` doesn't refuse a record push but files such a step after the merge with the same text at the top of its detail; `GlobService.get` marks an open step whose environment the board lacks with `unknownEnvironment` (carried by `compactStep`), which the glob view shows with a way out (dismiss it, add it again for one of the board's environments). The catalog agents (investigator, implementer, orchestrator, change_reviewer) carry the timing rule: environment-independent and one-shot is once; otherwise a step on the glob's dev environment with only `get_board` names (it can fall due before the merge), plus `downstream` when the command must also run as the change is released; after the merge only when the step needs the base to have the change; mixed commands are split and every command has its own `Run:` line; the reviewer flags misfiled steps `IN-SCOPE`.

**Rollout steps and Merge and continue (as built, s15t228).** A glob with rollout steps is merged by a person and stays open until they are done. *Landing:* `openRolloutSteps(stored)` counts the open, real rollout steps that hold back sign-off (kind `rollout`, `sign_off` in `blocks`, not the investigator's placeholder); the service passes it as `facts.rolloutSteps` (`CommandOptions.rollout`, `get`) and as `HumanStepFacts.rolloutSteps`. For a same with any, `merge` is withheld (a plain Merge is refused, and with it "merge when checks pass") and `merge_continue` is offered once the head's checks passed, titled "Merge and continue" (the derived Merge step's title says so too); it needs no implementation record and lands as a plain merge (rows 14 and 15), so the glob goes to Reviewing and is listed in Doing while steps are open, the steps are due, and the glob view puts them at the top as the checklist to run from master. A sub never merges itself with steps: writing a step converts it to a same (row 13d, escalation criterion `risk_path`, which outcome learning ignores like `size`; it cannot be de-escalated while the steps are open: `machine.deescalate` refuses a `risk_path` same with open steps, for the glob view, REST and MCP alike, and `GlobService.deescalate` loads the step count; a person's explicit `update({type: 'sub'})` is a deliberate type choice and is not checked). A super keeps Merge as its final merge and Merge and continue as its mid-way checkpoint (row 31). *Working it:* Pick up stays available on the open merged glob (row 9a), so `get_glob` in that session shows which steps are due; a step run by hand or early on the branch is told to the session, which ticks it with `complete_human_step` and an optional `note` (masked, kept in the step's event only), edits the list with `dismiss_human_step` (a reason) and `add_human_step`. *A failed step* starts the glob's next generation (row 31a) instead of a separate glob. Not built: a `rolling out` status or per-environment arming (superseded by the downstream step, s15t262; a merged glob with open steps is `reviewing` shown in Doing), and any change to `sstor`.

**A failed rollout step (s15b148).** When a run of a step fails, the agent or person reports it with `fail_human_step` or the glob view's "It failed" (step id and the error as `note`, masked). A rollout step of a merged glob in `reviewing` starts the glob's next generation instead (row 31a, s15t228). Any other step stays open and unmarked, the note is logged as an event, and the glob gets an open "Failed step" question, "this step failed: is there a fix glob?" (the one question the glob view still asks; a mentioned glob never gets one). The fix is a follow-up glob created with `holds` on the failed glob (gate `sign_off`, reason naming the step), so the original cannot be signed off until the fix has merged and rolled out. Nothing is created automatically.

**Sub review** runs as a GitHub Action and reports back to slop. It starts with basic checks (lint, type check, tests). Each board has a sensitive-paths setting, empty for now. A sub whose diff (against the base branch) exceeds the board's sub size limit also converts. **How the size is measured:** the changed lines of every file except generated ones. The left-out paths are the board's merge policy `sizeIgnoredPaths` (knowledge, changed through approved learnings; there is no board setting); with no merge policy, or an empty list, the built-in defaults apply: lockfiles (`pnpm-lock.yaml`, `package-lock.json`, `yarn.lock`) and `**/drizzle/meta/**`. The sensitive-path check still sees every file. The gate's reason says what was left out ("Changes 701 lines, not counting 3,047 generated (limit 1750)"), and the verdict records the counted lines (`changedLines`) and the left-out ones (`ignoredLines`). The learned limit's line counts read from a merge commit leave the same paths out. A flagged sub converts to a same. The gate's verdict (`SubReviewCompleted`) records why it converted (`cause`: size or sensitive), the lines the sub changes and the limit applied; verdicts recorded before these fields are read from their reason ("Changes N lines (limit L)").

The **sub size limit is learned from outcomes**, not set by admins (there is no manual override; the history makes every move auditable). It starts at 2,000 changed lines and stays between 200 and 10,000. It moves by an adaptive step (`boards.sub_limit_step`, 500 to start, between 100 and 2,000): an outcome in the same direction as the last move (raise after raise, lower after lower) grows the step x1.5, a reversal halves it. The move is the step scaled by how far off the sub was (a raise by its changed lines against the limit, 1 to 3; a lowering by its share of the limit, 1 at the near share up to 2) and by recency (an outcome older than the 14-day window counts less, linearly down to half), rounded to 50 lines; one lowering takes at most half of the limit. An hourly `board_jobs` job, `sub_limit` (under its lease, with Run now in the Subs panel of "What slop has learned" on the Knowledge page), reads the subs merged up to 28 days before its last successful run (the 14-day outcome window plus a 14-day margin; a failed run doesn't move that point), so an outcome in a window's last hour, or a candidate that was waiting when its window ended, is still decided on a later run, and records each sub's outcome once:

- *Merged unchanged (raises it):* the sub converted for its size, then merged with no commit after the conversion (pushes from superseded runs don't count) and no sign-off label moved to `added`. It is decided once the glob is signed off, or 14 days after the merge. The limit becomes `min(10000, L + move)`, for limit L.
- *Needed fixes after merging (lowers it):* the sub passed the gate and merged, and within 14 days of the merge either a sign-off label (FR, CR or QA) moved to `added`, or a bug glob created after the merge names the sub's ID in its title or summary and the findings model (Haiku, one call with a 30-second deadline) answers that the bug report says the defect was introduced by that sub, with a quote slop finds verbatim in the report and at least 4 words / 20 characters long. Naming a sub isn't blaming it, so a bug that only mentions it changes nothing. The limit becomes `max(200, L − move)`. A label outcome is used without asking the model. A passed sub whose verdict has no line count is measured from its merge commit (the code host's diff of the commit against its first parent, which for a squash merge is the whole PR; at most 300 files are counted); when that can't be read the outcome is recorded without a line count, since the count is evidence only. While the model is unavailable (or known to be down) bug references wait for the next run without calls. Answers that don't blame the sub are remembered by the bug report's text, so the same report isn't asked about again unless it is edited; a reference that gets no usable answer (unusable JSON or a timeout) 3 times is skipped and remembered too, and the run's result counts it.

Each outcome is recorded in `sub_limit_changes` (when, the limit before and after, the outcome, the sub, its changed lines (or unknown), the step used (before weighting) and the evidence: the conversion and merge, the label's checklist item, or the bug's ID and verified quote), once per sub and outcome; an outcome the rule doesn't move (the limit already at a bound, recorded with the limit after equal to the limit before) is still recorded, so it isn't checked again. The board write is conditional on the limit the job read, in the same transaction as the history row, under the board's sub-limit lock; settings saves don't write the limit. Outcomes found in one run apply in order, each to the limit the one before left. The gate reads the board's limit when it decides, so a decision uses the limit of that moment, and a later move doesn't change it. Board settings show the limit ("Sub size limit: N changed lines (learned from outcomes)", read-only) with its history: date, from → to, the step, why, the glob and the evidence, as plain text, and the board's current step. **When the limit moves:** a raise always applies, but a sub that needed fixes lowers the limit only when it was near it: its changed lines were at least half the current limit (`SUB_LIMIT_NEAR_SHARE`, beside the step). A smaller sub, or one whose size is unknown, says nothing about the limit (a 2-line sub needing a fix is not a size problem): its outcome is recorded with the limit after equal to the limit before, and evidence saying it was too small to say anything about the limit. A sub of known size that needed fixes while small is instead a signal for the board's risk areas (below).

**Risk areas (as built, s15f46)** are the board's kinds of work that need a person's review, which intake reads with the learned sub size: it chooses same only when the request's files match an area or the work is clearly beyond the size. Each board has rows in `board_risk_areas` (name, path patterns, why, source default or learned, strength, clean count), seeded lazily from the five defaults (auth and security; migrations that rewrite existing data; agent instructions; infrastructure and production; public API or cross-board contracts), with their history in `risk_area_changes` (area, added, strengthened, clean, removed, reset or correction; the glob; the cause). Outcomes apply to the paths of the sub (its merge commit's files, else the paths its plan named), once per glob and cause: a sub that was reverted (`MergeReverted`), a failed deploy after the merge (`DeployFailed`), a bug that blamed it (the sub-limit learning's verified quote) or a small sub that needed fixes strengthens every area its paths fall under and adds a learned area (`Learned: <directory>`) for paths no area covers; a person changing intake's type counts three times, sub to same as above and same to sub as clean. A sub merged 14 days with none of those, no bug naming it and no recorded fix counts clean for the areas its paths fall under; a learned area with 3 clean outcomes is removed, a default one is only marked sub-safe (intake leans sub there), never removed. Board settings show the areas (learned or default) with their history and causes, and admins reset them to the defaults (`GET /api/boards/{b}/risk-areas`, `POST /api/boards/{b}/risk-areas/reset`). Cross-board proposals are a manual process and not part of the code.

**Escalation (s15f48):** a glob created through MCP is a sub unless the request says same, it clearly needs a human decision before work starts, or it is clearly a big task; an explicit `type` always wins. The sub gate's size limit is only a high backstop (a new board starts at 5,000 lines; boards keep the value they have learned), and agents judge instead: the implementer (through the orchestrator) and the change_reviewer name a need for a human when the change makes a design decision the plan didn't settle, touches a risk area, rewrites existing data, changes auth, permissions or secrets, weakens or deletes tests, changes agent instructions, grew well beyond the plan, or leaves in-scope review findings open after the allowed rounds. The orchestrator then calls `escalate_to_same` (rows 13a, 13b). **Escalation block (s15t200):** the glob view says it in one plain line, "Changed from sub to same because <reason>.", with who or what did it and when, and the evidence collapsed under "Details". The size check never escalates. **Escalation outcome learning (s15f55):** each escalation names its criterion (`ESCALATION_CRITERIA`; read from the reason when absent) and an hourly pass (the `intake_outcome` job) records every outcome once per glob in `escalation_records`, with its cause: escalated and merged with nothing asked of it (signed off, or the 14-day window passed) is `unnecessary` (-1); a de-escalation by a person is `unnecessary` (-3); escalated and then a sign-off label asking for changes, or a revert, is `justified` (+1); a sub that merged unescalated and was reverted or named by a bug in its window is `missed` (+2), and a person turning its follow-up into a same is `missed` (+3, classified by the paths of the merge or plan). A criterion's score (since the last reset) gives its stance: `loosened` at -1 or below, `strengthened` at +1 or above, else the default. A change of stance submits an `agent-behaviour` learning to the board's KB for an admin to approve (never edited directly), and the outcomes reach the risk areas (a risk-area escalation approved unchanged counts clean, one that got changes counts as blocked, a de-escalation counts as a person's change to sub; missed ones already arrive through the revert and bug learning). Board settings show each criterion as learned or default with the history, and admins reset them (the history stays).

The **size check** (as built, s15f28; advisory since s15f144) is the same idea for globs that are bigger than one PR, whatever their type. **It advises and never holds:** a flagged glob can start at once (a sub or an auto-triggered same starts as usual; a flagged sub stays a sub), and the flag stays as information. *Estimate:* when a glob is created, and when its plan.md is saved while it is in Planning, intake estimates its size from the plan's text (tasks: checkbox lines, and list lines under a Tasks heading; numbered lines under other headings, such as an investigation list, are not tasks; "Done when" lines; the areas it touches (migration, server, web, CLI, LLM, infra); sections with text and no task) and asks the intake model, in one structured call, for the number of independent parts the work falls into and for how many of them could be built at the same time once any shared contract is in place (`parallelParts`). The estimate is stored with its evidence in `glob_size_checks` (one row per glob). Without the model (unavailable, slow, unusable answer, a plan under 200 characters, or a glob created with a summary and no plan: a sentence says nothing about how many parts the work has, so such a glob is never flagged by the model's count) the estimate comes from the text alone (about three tasks to a part; a section with no task is context and never a part; no parallelism is known): creating a glob never waits on or fails with the model. *Flag:* a glob is **oversized** when its tasks exceed the board's threshold or its independent parts do, **and** at least `minParallel` of its parts (the board's fan-out floor, starting at 2) could be built at the same time. Task count alone never flags: a 10-task serial plan stays whole, and so does any plan judged only from its text or whose model answer leaves `parallelParts` out (parallel work that gains no parallelism adds nothing). A proposal whose workstreams would wait on each other in one line (critical path equal to the workstream count: a chain has no parallel workstreams) is dropped and the glob is not flagged, with the evidence saying why. The threshold starts at more than 5 tasks or 3 parts with a fan-out floor of 2 (`size_thresholds`, columns `max_tasks`, `max_parts`, `min_parallel`; no row means the default). *Learning:* the hourly `size_threshold` board job (Run now in the Size panel of "What slop has learned" on the Knowledge page; reuses the `intake_outcome` job's `glob_outcomes`) reads each merged glob that has a size check once its outcome is final (14 days after the merge; bug globs are left out). A glob flagged that did not fan out and merged cleanly (review rounds below the risk tier's maximum, no failed run, PR open time within twice the board's median PR time, and no bug naming it within 14 days) is `kept_whole_clean` (read as "flagged and merged cleanly"): the threshold goes up one step. A glob that was not flagged and struggled (review rounds at the maximum, a failed run, or a PR open more than three times the median) is `unflagged_struggled`: it goes down one step. A flagged glob that did not fan out and merged but was not clean is `flag_confirmed` and leaves it. Whether a glob fanned out is read from the latest `WorkstreamsRecorded` event of its implementation record (`fannedOut`), not from a split: a `GlobSplit` is a scope cut and changes no outcome. *Fan-out outcomes (s15f152):* a glob whose block says it fanned out gets one of two outcomes instead of the two above, unless it was unflagged and struggled (that stays `unflagged_struggled`, fanned out or not, since its limits were too high). `fanout_helped`: it merged cleanly by the same test and no workstream failed, was retried (`attempts` above 1) or conflicted on integration; the fan-out floor `minParallel` goes down one step (parallel work paid off, so smaller parallelism is flagged too). `fanout_stalled`: it did not merge cleanly, or a workstream failed, was retried or conflicted; the floor goes up one step. Only the floor moves on these; the task and part limits move on the other outcomes only. The PR time rules apply once the board has 5 merged PRs. A step is one task and one part together, each within its bounds (tasks 2 to 12, parts 2 to 6) and the floor moves by one step within 2 to 4; every outcome is recorded once per board and glob in `size_threshold_changes` (when, the threshold before and after (`from_parallel` / `to_parallel` for the floor), the outcome, the glob, the evidence; a fan-out outcome's evidence names the workstreams and what stalled them) and shown in the Size panel of "What slop has learned", where the history reads tasks / parts / parallel (`GET /api/boards/{b}/size-threshold`). The old outcomes `split_stalled` and `split_helped` (a split's parts judged together once every part had merged) are no longer recorded and move nothing; rows already recorded stay readable in the history. *Proposal, as workstreams:* for a flagged glob the model proposes **workstreams**, shares of the work that the one run can build at the same time inside the glob's one PR: each has a `name`, a `goal` (one sentence), the `files` it is expected to change, `plan` (its share of plan.md, copied verbatim) and `needs` (indexes of earlier workstreams it builds on). The model is asked to make a shared schema or contract the first workstream so each consumer needs only it; a section with no task (Evidence, Why, Notes, an investigation list) never becomes a workstream: it is copied into every workstream that doesn't already carry it, and a proposal left with fewer than two workstreams is dropped. Needs are cut down to direct prerequisites (a workstream needing the first through another doesn't name the first too). Sharing a file path relates no workstreams. The proposal also carries `criticalPath` (workstreams on its longest chain of needs) and `maxParallel` (the most buildable at once). The proposal built from the forecast's modules and the one from the investigation's `parts` have the same shape. A check stored before s15f144 holds `parts` (`title`, `plan`, `after`) in `glob_size_checks.proposal`; the store reads them as workstreams (name from the title, no files). *Board:* the card shows an Oversized chip while the glob is flagged and in Planning, and the glob view explains the flag and lists the workstreams; there is no Keep whole, no Propose again and no Split as proposed: **Split** (the scope cut) is in the glob's menu like for any glob in Planning. *Display:* the Size panel shows the learned threshold in plain words beside the default, with Reset to defaults for admins (`POST /api/boards/{b}/size-threshold/reset`, a `reset` entry in the history). *Routines:* a flagged sub, and a flagged same with `autoTrigger`, is created as usual (nothing holds it for the flag; a sub that waits for a glob still waits for that). *A flagged glob keeps its type (s15t200, replaces s15f136's size escalation):* the size check is advice for the run (workstreams build a big glob's parts inside one PR) and never changes a glob's type: a sub stays a sub, at creation and when a plan save re-judges the check, with no escalation, no `Escalated` event and no card or glob-view block for the flag beyond the Oversized chip. `GlobService.reconcileSizeCheck` only investigates a glob that now looks big. The criterion `size` stays in `ESCALATION_CRITERIA` so old events still read, but nothing raises it. Migration 0076 turned the open globs that the old rule had escalated (criterion `size`) back into subs and cleared the escalation; merged ones keep it as history. A split (a scope cut) takes the original's type for its parts as for any glob. `get_glob` carries `oversized` and `sizeCheck` with the workstreams summarised (name, goal, files, needs, a short `planSummary` and its `planChars`; there is no `include`), and `get_context` carries `size` with the workstreams in full and `minParallel`, the board's current fan-out floor (the orchestrator fans out only when at least that many workstreams can run at the same time). A plan saved in Planning (the board editor, `save_plan`, `PUT /globs/{id}/plan`) re-judges the check, and `GlobService.reconcileSizeCheck` then turns a waiting sub it flags into a same (the check's `onRechecked` hook). Known limits: a plan edited into oversize after the glob started is flagged but a run already started is not stopped. The routes `POST /globs/{id}/keep-whole` and `POST /globs/{id}/actions/repropose-split`, `SizeCheckService.keepWhole` and the decision `kept_whole` are gone (migration 0071 cleared the old decisions).

**Size forecast (as built, s15f50):** the size check also forecasts the work's footprint, so the flag and the proposed workstreams follow the code and not only the plan's words. *Forecast:* in the background after a glob is created (creation never waits; `GlobService.create` calls `SizeCheckService.refresh`) and when plan.md is saved in Planning, a capped model loop reads the board's repository through the code view (`GitCodeView`, base branch, read-only; at most 6 code-view calls: at most 5 tree or grep looks, then the `touchedModules` read) and predicts the files the plan will change. From them slop derives the modules (one part per module, with the part's file set and which parts it `needs`, from the importers `touchedModules` finds), the fan-in, whether it touches a migration or a public API, the risk areas and risk tier, and a lines range. The forecast is stored as `forecast` inside the check's `estimate` jsonb (no column), with its `source` (`code`, or `text` when only the plan's text was available), `confidence` (low, medium, high) and evidence lines (shown with the check's evidence). It is only written when the footprint has at least one file, the plan is at least 200 characters and the plan hash still matches when it finishes. *Reference class:* (reading only the picked globs' outcomes and events, never the board's whole history) the plan is embedded (the embedder) and the nearest merged globs' change summaries (up to 5 distinct globs, relevance at least 0.25) give a reference class: each sample's files, lines, review rounds, rework runs, escalation, follow-up bugs, areas and whether it was a clean sub, plus a one-line summary. The forecast blends three signals, the code's, the reference class's and the plan text's, with the board's calibration weights (start: code 0.6, reference 0.3, text 0.1), then scales files and lines by the mean of the calibration's factors for the areas it touches. *Flag with a forecast:* when a forecast exists it replaces the text rule, under the same principle (size alone never flags): the forecast's parts form a graph through their needs, and the glob is oversized only when at least 2 parts can be built at the same time (`splitShape`'s `maxParallel`; a core -> server -> web chain is one line of needs and stays whole) **and** either the parts number the board's part limit or more (never fewer than 3, so the learned floor of 2 cannot flag every two-module change; each part is a module, so each is already a split point) or its calibrated file count makes more units of work (4 files each) than the task limit. The forecast's part limit therefore reads "at least `maxParts` parts" where the text rule reads "more than `maxParts`": the estimate from the code is the better measure, so a change that spans three unrelated modules is flagged at the default of 3. The reasons say which, and that N parts could be built at the same time. The proposal's workstreams then come from the forecast's part file sets (one per module, with its `files` and a `goal` naming them; the plan's tasks that name a workstream's files or module go to it, the rest to the first, context sections go to every workstream, a workstream needs the ones it directly builds on, and the proposal carries `criticalPath` and `maxParallel`), and is dropped for a chain, as for the model's proposal. A predicted path the model's looks cannot place (not seen in a tree or grep result, and its directory never listed) is dropped before it counts, so a model that invents paths gets no forecast. One forecast runs per glob at a time: a call that arrives meanwhile asks for one more pass after it. A glob that starts before the background forecast finishes (a sub released at once) is never forecast: a glob outside Planning is left alone. *Fallback:* with no code view, no embedder, no model, an unreadable repository or any failure, the forecast is absent (or has no reference class) and the text estimate and its flag stand unchanged; the forecast never blocks or fails a create or a save. *Three-point record:* when the `size_threshold` job reads a merged glob's final outcome that has a forecast, it records once per glob in `glob_size_records` the three points: the forecast, the investigation's correction (the files the glob's parsed investigation artifact lists, or none) and the actual (files from the change summary's Files line when there is one, changed lines, review rounds, rework runs, whether it was escalated, follow-up bugs, hours open), and `workstreams`: when the glob's latest implementation record has a valid workstreams block (s15f146), each workstream with its predicted and actual files, state and conflicts, set against the forecast part that shares most of its actual files (that part's name, how many of the actual files it had, how many it missed, how many it predicted that were not changed; none when no part shares a file; null when the record has no valid block, absent on records made before workstreams; no migration, it lives in the `actual` jsonb). *Calibration:* each record moves the board's `size_calibrations` row (one row per board, jsonb state: weights, per-area factors, samples): an area's factor moves 30% of the way toward the actual lines over the raw estimated lines (clamped to 0.5 to 3; moves under 0.05 are not recorded), and when the file count disagreed with the signals by at least 25% the weights move 0.05 from the worst signal (floor 0.05) to the closest. The file count is the union of the actual files of the record's done workstreams when it has any (what the run really built, so the forecast's module/part split is calibrated against the workstreams it used), else the investigation's list; the weight move's cause then names the workstreams and where the forecast's split missed ("the forecast part P covered 3 of the 5 files of workstream W", or "no forecast part covered workstream W"), and an area-factor move's cause names the workstreams the glob was built as. Every move is a row in `size_calibration_changes` (when, the glob, area or weights, the value before and after, the cause as text), once per board, glob and cause, under the `size_threshold` job's lock; the job's result carries the number of records and moves (`calibration`). Migration 0043 adds the three tables. *Where it shows:* settings show the calibration read-only beside the size threshold (weights, per-area factors against the defaults, the history with causes: `GET /api/boards/{b}/size-calibration`, members); an oversized glob's view shows the forecast (expected files and modules, lines range, parts with their file sets, risk, confidence, the reference class's samples and the calibrated factors and weights). `get_context`'s `size` carries the forecast (files, modules, lines, parts, risk, confidence and `history`, the reference class's one-line summary, with a note that it is a prediction to confirm or correct) as the investigator's starting point; the reference samples and the calibration are shown in the glob view and settings, not in `get_context`; the files the investigation then lists are what the calibration compares the signals against. *Not done:* the forecast does not yet move a glob's type (sub or same) or the sub gate's limit, and the calibration does not yet learn the part count or the risk tier.

**Agent side (s15f143, replaces s15t44's split step):** a run no longer splits a big glob into board globs. People size a glob for review (one glob, one PR, one QA, one sign-off); the agents divide the work inside the run as **workstreams**, which never appear on the board. The investigator's `## Size` is `## Workstreams` (W0, W1, …: a goal, files, its share of plan.md and its needs; a shared contract is W0; every shared or hotspot file belongs to W0 or exactly one workstream, and two workstreams that would edit the same file are merged), starting from `size.forecast`'s parts; the investigation block's `parts` are the workstreams. The orchestrator's Phase 1 size step only advises: it never calls `split_glob`, and offers a scope cut (an independent follow-up glob in group `workstreams`) to a person only when a workstream could ship alone and nothing needs it, never in a routine. Between Phases 2 and 3 it decides whether to fan out (at least 2 workstreams that can run at once and are each worth an agent; a chain stays sequential) and writes a `## Workstreams` status table (not started / running / done / waiting on decision / failed) into the implementation record. Phase 3 runs W0 first on the glob's branch, then one implementer per parallel workstream, launched together with `isolation: worktree` from the branch after W0 and given only its brief (the implementer's Mode C: its files, the W0 contract, the files it must not touch; it commits `<id>: <workstream>` in its worktree and never pushes). Each finished workstream is integrated onto the glob's branch in dependency order and pushed (the pre-push check applies), and the table updated, so a take-over, `--from 3` or a re-run runs only the workstreams not done. A failed workstream is retried once; a second failure fails the run naming the workstream. A mid-run decision is a `suggest_decision` plus a `merge` relation `until: decided` on it: the other workstreams finish and the run ends waiting. Review fixes go to one implementer, or fan out the same way when the findings span workstreams. Phases 4 to 6 run once on the integrated branch. **Workstream rules (s15t169):** changing W0 mid-run is the orchestrator's alone, as a `W0 amend` commit, with done dependants marked `recheck` (fast checks, re-brief only on failure), running ones re-briefed, recorded under Decisions, and at most 2 per run before the rest is built sequentially; a `## Needs from other workstreams` request is handled once per (requester, file) (owner not started: added to its brief; running: sent when it returns; done: the orchestrator edits or moves the file), and a second request for the same file between the same pair merges the two workstreams; after integration the full checks run once before Phase 4 and a failure goes to the owner of the failing files as a fix brief on the branch (several or no owner: one implementer with the whole diff), recording which combination broke; the investigator may mark `uses` (calls code another adds without a shared contract), run in parallel against an interface written into both briefs and integrated user-after-provider; and a workstream is worth an agent at 2 or more tasks or 3 or more files until the learned floor in `size` exists. The board's own `.claude/agents` copies and its "Splitting oversized globs" board rule follow through the next agent set and `submit_learning`; the board-side hold and split sign-off gate are gone (s15f144): a split is only a scope cut.

**Pre-push check (s15b161):** the `pre_push_checks.sh` PreToolUse hook (Bash) runs before any `git push` and blocks it (exit 2, the failing lines on stderr) when the fast checks fail. It runs `.sstor/checks.sh <changed files>` when the repo has that script (a non-zero exit blocks); otherwise eslint on the changed TS/JS files, `tsc --noEmit` for each changed package with a `tsconfig.json`, and vitest once per package for its changed test files (run in the main shell, so a failure sets the exit status). `--no-verify` is refused. A person can still push themselves; the hook only gates routines and sessions. `catalog/scripts/pre-push-checks.test.sh` covers a failing lint check, a failing and a passing changed test, and one vitest run per package.

**Workstreams block (s15f146, `domain/workstreams.ts`):** the implementation record of a fanned-out run ends its `## Workstreams` section with one fenced ```` ```workstreams ```` block of strict JSON, so the learning loops read data and not the status table: `fannedOut`, `maxParallel` (an integer of at least 1, no more than the number of workstreams) and `workstreams`, each with `name` (unique), `goal`, `predictedFiles`, `actualFiles`, `needs` (names of other workstreams, no cycles), `state` (`not_started`, `running`, `done`, `waiting_decision`, `failed` or `skipped`), `attempts`, `conflicts` (files that conflicted at integration) and `durationMinutes` (an integer or null); `fannedOut` true needs at least 2 workstreams. Unknown keys are ignored. *Parse:* `addArtifact` (the one write path) parses the last such fence of every `implementation_plan` version, as it does the investigation block. A record with no `## Workstreams` section and no block (a small glob) is silent. A section without a block, or a block that does not validate, is stored as text with the reasons in the artifact's provenance `validation` ("Workstreams block not parsed: ..."), never refused, and records nothing. *Event:* a valid block that differs from the previous record version's block appends `WorkstreamsRecorded` in the same transaction, under the glob's artifact lock (data: the parsed block, `version`, `commitSha`, `runId`); the same block pushed again appends nothing, so consumers read the latest event per glob. The catalog orchestrator writes and updates the block with the status table, and the implementer's Mode C summary reports what the block needs (files, conflicts, retries, time). Later parts of this work read the event: fan-out outcomes for the size threshold, per-workstream forecast calibration, integration conflicts for hotspots and recurring workstream patterns for learnings.

**Workstream conflicts feed the hotspots (s15f154, `domain/conflict-record.ts`, `domain/hotspots.ts`, `app/hotspot-decisions.ts`):** the files a workstream conflicted on when it was integrated (the block's `conflicts`) are conflicts in the same lane as those between globs. `conflictRecords` adds one row per workstream and file from each glob's latest `WorkstreamsRecorded` event (`workstream` set to its name, flagged and resolved at the event's time, resolved by the run, kind unknown), and the hotspot job reads that event type with the conflict events. `detectHotspots` counts each workstream that conflicted on a file as one more party beside the globs, so a file that three workstreams of one glob collided on is a hotspot like one three globs collided on; a hotspot carries `workstreamConflicts` (absent when none). When such a file recurs, `hotspot-decisions` proposes splitting it as for any hotspot, and tells the model and the decision's Conflicts section how many of the conflicts were between workstreams. Nothing is stored; a block pushed again replaces the glob's earlier rows, so a file stops being hot when its conflicts leave the window.

**Webhooks are best effort; reconciliation is the source of truth.** GitHub doesn't retry a failed delivery, so a restart or a tunnel blip can lose a push, check, ready or merge event and leave a glob waiting for nothing. The `reconcile` job (`SLOP_JOBS`; session servers don't run it by default) re-reads open PRs from the code host. On start it queues a `reconcile_pr` outbox effect (generation-checked, idempotent) for every glob with an open PR (in Doing, Implementing, In review or Merging); every 10 minutes it queues one, at most 25 per tick, for each glob that looks stuck: a ready PR with no check result for its current head that hasn't changed for over 10 minutes, or a glob a run is watching that hasn't changed for over an hour (`updatedAt` stands in for the last webhook, since every webhook that matters changes the glob). The executor reads the PR (open, draft or ready; merged with its merge sha; closed; head sha) and applies what was missed through the transitions the webhooks use: `merged`, `prClosed`, `commitPushed`, `prReadyForReview`, and `checksChanged` (which queues `refresh_checks`) when the head has no result. **The merge line:** the head of a board's merge line with no check result for its current head for over 20 minutes (counted from its update or push) is rechecked once (`mergeHeadRechecked`: `refresh_checks` and `reconcile_pr`); if there is still no result 20 minutes after that, the glob leaves the line with the reason (row 16) and the next one goes. Waiting globs hold no work and are not swept for it. The sweep also calls `advance_merge_line` for a board whose line has globs but no head, so a lost nudge or a restart mid-transition can't leave the line stopped. Each catch-up is logged (`reconcile: s15f9 head moved to … (missed webhook)`); it raises no notification beyond what the transition itself raises. A second run with nothing missed changes nothing.

**Merging**

- Squash merge only. The commit message starts with the glob ID, e.g. `s1t4: Fix login timeout`; PR titles use the same format.
- Repository rules enforce the merge guarantees for every entry point (GitHub UI, gh, sstor, slop): required checks must pass on the PR's current head commit and the branch must be up to date with master, so a stale passing result can never merge newer changes. Slop's GitHub App is on the ruleset's bypass list, so GitHub does not enforce this for slop's own merges: slop enforces it itself. Immediately before it calls the merge API it reads the PR and merges only a head that is the checked `sha` (pinned in the call), is not behind the base and whose check runs passed (none failed or unfinished, at least one succeeded). Only the head of the board's merge line is merged or updated: see the merge line paragraph below. A behind head is never merged as it is, whatever its checks say: `behind` is a flag apart from the check result, and a behind head's `headChecks` is its own runs' result (pending or failed, not passed). A head whose checks are not green is not merged (it is retried while pending, and fails the merge when failed). For merges slop starts (subs and the Merge button), slop updates the branch, waits for the checks and squash-merges; a conflict (found when updating the branch, or reported by GitHub before it) leaves the glob failed in Doing with the reason "Merge conflict with `<base>`", never as failing checks. Resolve conflict on such a glob (row 20a), or on one flagged as below, posts one PR comment that mentions `@claude` (needs both the Claude GitHub App and a workflow that runs `anthropics/claude-code-action`, both board readiness items; it works in GitHub Actions with no local pull) and starts no routine run. The comment gives the glob's and the merged glob's ID, title and summary, the conflicting files, and the instructions: merge `origin/<base>` into the branch, resolve keeping both intents, run lint, type check and tests, push. It carries a marker with the request time, so a retried effect never comments twice. Merges done anywhere else are observed through the merged event.
- **The merge line (s15t255):** slop's own merge train, one line per board (GitHub's merge queue isn't used: it needs an organisation plan, and slop has one merge mechanism).
