The line is the board's `merging` globs that have `mergeLine` (`joinedAt`, and `head` for the one at the front); nothing else stores the order, so a glob that leaves `merging` by any path (merged, closed, cancelled, failed, deleted) is out of the line by definition. **Joining:** a glob joins when it is ready to merge: a sub whose checks and review passed with its merge armed, a same whose Merge was pressed (row 14), a super's Merge and continue, a retried merge (row 16c, at the back) or an agent-set glob; the order is first come, first served by `joinedAt` (then glob ID). A glob held by a `merge` relation or an open question is not in the line: it stays in `pr_open` (row 14b) and joins at release time, at the back, so it never blocks the globs behind it; a gate that appears after a glob joined sends it back to `pr_open` held when it would become the head. **Only the head works.** `advance_merge_line` (an outbox effect for the board, written in the transaction of every join and every leave) calls `GlobService.advanceMergeLine`, which under a per-board lock promotes at most one glob to head (`MergeLineHead`; a partial unique index on the head backs the lock, so two heads can never exist). The head updates its branch from the base once if it is behind (the base is the repo's default branch, read from GitHub, never a board setting), waits for that head's checks, then merges (squash, `sha` pinned to the checked head, with the safety checks above); then the next glob becomes the head. Red checks, a conflict or a failed merge make the head leave the line with the reason (rows 16 and 16a) and the next one goes. The others wait: no update, no checks (their own checks from their last push still count for joining). A push to a waiting glob keeps its place and its new head must pass before it is merged; a push to the head restarts its wait for green. **Cancel merge** takes a glob out of the line (row 14c; the glob view's ▾ only). **What people see:** through `presentGlob`, the head's top-right status reads "merging" (or "running checks" while its checks run); the others read "next to merge" (second) or "N ahead in the merge line" (third and later), with the hourglass icon and the IDs ahead in the tooltip. Nothing is shown bottom left of the card for a glob in the line (the Merge that put it there is done). The place is computed on the server from the board's merging globs (the card can't see the others). A run that is only watching a merging glob is never a "no progress" handover (s15t226: a glob being merged, or armed to merge, is not stalled). A stuck head and a line without a head are repaired by the reconcile sweep (see Webhooks are best effort). Slop's App stays on the ruleset's bypass list for emergencies and for reverts.
- **Conflicts flagged after each merge:** when a merge to the base branch is observed (row 15, or Merge and continue), slop rechecks the board's other open PRs (`pr_open`, or `in_progress` with a draft PR) through the outbox: a `check_conflict` effect per glob that retries while GitHub has not computed mergeability. A glob that now conflicts records `conflict` (base, files, the glob that merged, time) and its card shows "Conflicts with `<base>` since `<id>` merged: `<files>`" next to the other stuck hints; the glob does not fail. A push to a glob with a flagged conflict rechecks it, and the flag clears once the PR can merge again. A conflict is never recorded as failed checks: the head keeps its last real result (a conflicting PR usually has no check runs, so a stale empty failed result is dropped), and the readiness item for the Claude GitHub App counts only real failures (a failed check run on the head with log lines or a link, no conflict, 15 minutes without a push or slop call from the watching run), naming the commit and link. Resolve conflict starts a routine run (purpose `resolve_conflict`) that merges the base branch into the glob's branch, resolves additive conflicts, runs the checks and pushes; a real clash of behaviour goes to `report_failure` with the decision needed. It is offered while a run is only watching (the watching run is ended and replaced) and again after a failed attempt; a queued or active run blocks it. After a failed attempt the card shows `sstor --glob <id> --take-over`. The Claude GitHub App's workflow is not used for conflicts: it has no git tools and isn't widened. The reconcile sweep repairs a conflicted glob whose head checks were recorded failed with no detail. For a human implementer the card and Resolve conflict show `sstor --glob <id> --take-over` (`sstor --glob <id>` for the implementer), with the git commands and `sstor --glob <id> --resolve` (mergetool only, no Claude) under "Resolve by hand instead (advanced)" and slop posts no comment. When the comment was posted and the PR still conflicts 15 minutes later, the card adds "Resolve locally: `sstor --glob <id> --take-over`".
- Every PR (sub, same or super) runs the repo's `checks` workflow (install, lint, type check, tests) on its head, including the head after slop updates the branch; the `checks` check is required on master by a `slop` ruleset (`setup-repo.sh --protect`: required checks on an up-to-date head, no force push or deletion; slop's GitHub App and repo admins are on its bypass list, because slop gates and reverts subs itself and classic branch protection can't exempt an App), and it is also the check a sub waits for: there is no separate sub-gate workflow, and a check run counts only for the PR head it ran on, so a `checks` run from a push to the base branch never stands in. Slop's merge waits on GitHub's merge state, so a same or super whose head hasn't passed `checks` stays pending, and a failing one fails, as for subs.
- **Check states:** a head's check runs are *passed*, *pending* or *failed*. A **cancelled** run is none of the failures: it has no verdict, so it leaves the head pending (and the gate check lookup ignores it) while a newer run on the same head replaces it, as when a push supersedes a run in progress. If no newer run has come after 5 minutes, slop asks the GitHub App to re-request the cancelled run (best effort; only the app that created a run can) and keeps waiting. A cancelled run is never counted as a failed check.
- **Failed checks are explained:** when slop records failed head checks (`checksCompleted`, from a check event or a merge's update), it reads the commit's check runs through the `CodeHost` port and stores a short failure on the head checks: the first failing check's name, its failed step (the Actions job's steps), the first error lines from the last ~60 lines of the job log (a check run's ID is its job's ID for Actions) and the run's link. Lines are the log's error lines, not a summary; where the log can't be read, the run's own output is used, else only the check's name. The card shows "checks failed" with the step and first error on hover, the glob view's disabled Merge says the same, and a merge that failed on checks after updating the branch carries it in its reason. This is the first piece of the build-failure summaries (slice 7); an LLM summary of a long tail comes later through the LLM port.
- **A red base branch:** slop keeps the latest check result for the head of the board's base branch (`baseChecks`: head SHA, passed or failed, the failing check as above, the glob whose merge turned it red and when). A check finishing on the base branch (`check_run` or `check_suite` webhooks for it) queues one idempotent `refresh_base_checks` effect per board, which reads the branch head's checks and the head commit's subject (`<id>: <title>`, which names the glob that merged). A glob whose head fails the same check with the same first error as a red base is marked `inheritedFrom` the base (in either order: the mark is also set on the globs already failed when the base turns red, and cleared when the base no longer fails like that). Its card says "`<base>` is red (since `<id>`): not this glob's change", `get_glob` returns `failedChecks` with the same text and `headChecks.inheritedFrom`, and the board shows a banner naming the glob whose merge turned the base red while it is red. A failure inherited from the base is not "stuck": the routine isn't told its run hasn't reacted. The catalog orchestrator, in unattended mode, doesn't fix an inherited failure in the glob's branch: it waits and records it in the run's notes.
- **Re-run when the base is fixed:** when the base branch's head goes from red to green, slop queues an `update_branch` effect for every open glob (`pr_open`, or `in_progress` with a PR) whose failed head checks are inherited, generation-checked and idempotent (dropped when the glob has been pushed to since or no longer has an inherited failure). It updates the PR branch with the base through GitHub's update-branch call (the one a merge uses); the push resets the head checks and they run again on the fixed base. A conflict found by the update is flagged on the glob like any other conflict; a branch that is already up to date just loses the stale mark.
- Branch protection requires passing checks but no approvals; direct pushes to master are not allowed. For sames and supers, merging is the approval: the developer reviews the results on the glob and clicks Merge (on the glob or in GitHub). Slop merges subs once the sub gate passes.
- GitHub's "automatically delete head branches" setting is on. No other git cleanup for now.

**GitHub App**

- Slop reaches the repository through a `CodeHost` port (provision, labels, close, reopen, delete branch, merge state, squash merge); the GitHub App is its only adapter, and a host-specific webhook adapter turns deliveries into the same state-machine events. Another host (GitLab, Forgejo) would mean a new adapter pair, but routines and Claude's auto-fix only work with GitHub today, so that is the real lock-in.

- Slop's GitHub operations (branches, PRs, merges, check results) use the app's installation tokens, acting as `slop[bot]`. The private key lives in Secrets Manager (`slop/prod/github-app`, written by the manifest flow).
- The app is on the `slop` ruleset's bypass list, so it can merge subs to master before their required checks finish; slop gates them itself and reverts one that turns master red.
- One board maps to one repo.

**Webhooks:** the app subscribes to `push`, `pull_request`, `pull_request_review`, `pull_request_review_comment`, `pull_request_review_thread`, `issue_comment`, `check_run` and `check_suite` (an App made before `pull_request_review_thread` was added shows the readiness item until an admin ticks it under Permissions & events; the weekly poll covers `/wrong` replies and reactions meanwhile). A webhook adapter verifies the signature, reads the glob ID from the branch and calls the core, which records commits, PR ready and closed, merges, check results and review comments, and starts the glob's deploy when a push arrives and an environment is set. The glob stores references only. No reconciliation job; missed deliveries are redelivered by hand from the app settings.

**Review and CI results** attach to the glob through the branch name, with or without a PR. The sub review and other Actions report as GitHub check runs; slop receives them through the GitHub App's existing check webhooks and reads summaries and annotations from the Checks API. No slop credential is stored in the repo, and a crashed Action still arrives as a failed check. A REST callback remains for results that are not GitHub checks. The MCP and REST endpoints are separate driving adapters on the same application services.

## Review and testing

The repo's build scripts decide which checks run, using the PR labels; slop displays results on the glob. Every glob in Doing has a PR, opened as a draft by slop's GitHub App when the glob enters Doing, so CI, reviewers, labels and auto-fix can attach (after a super's Merge and continue, its next draft PR opens on the next push). Because GitHub cannot open a PR on a branch identical to master, slop creates each glob's branch with an empty first commit (\<id>: start), which the squash merge removes.

| # | Stage | Where | Sub | Same | Super | Blocks merge? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 | Lint, type check, unit tests | Local (routine or sessionator) | Before PR is ready | Before PR is ready | Before ready for review | No (self-check) |
| L2 | Local AI review against plan and conventions, run by the implementer (routine or sessionator) on every project; pushed to slop with put\_artifact as the local review artifact and shown under its own local review icon on the card, separate from the remote review, to support the human CR | Local | Light | Full | Developer-triggered | No (self-check) |
| R1 | CI: lint, type check, unit and integration tests | CodeBuild or Actions, reported as checks | Yes | Yes | Yes | **Yes** |
| R2 | Sub gate: static checks, size, sensitive paths, scope vs plan | GitHub Action | Yes | — | — | Converts to same if flagged |
| R3 | Independent AI code review of the changed code only: logic errors, newly introduced bugs, departures from conventions and patterns. A reviewer on the PR can fetch the board's review guide with `get_review_guide`. Slop adds the plan summary to the PR description | GitHub app on the PR | Yes, non-blocking; findings may arrive after merge | Yes | Yes | No |
| R4 | Security and privacy review of the changed code: vulnerabilities, and privacy meaning behaviour changes that expose user data | Part of R3 or a separate check | Via R3 | Yes | Yes | No; developer decides |
| R5 | Deploy to the glob's environment (ATF runs after the merge is deployed to int, started by the int pipeline) | CodeBuild | Slop deploys the branch on each push to the glob's dev environment (every new code glob is given one) | Slop deploys the branch to the glob's environment on each push, if one is set | Deployed on each push to the developer's environment | No (flagged) |
| R6 | Merge | Slop (subs) or developer | Slop, after R1 and R2 | Developer | Developer | — |
| — | FR, CR, QA sign-off | Board | QA | FR, CR, QA | FR, CR, QA | Post-merge |

**Independent review: publishing (as built, s15f42).** *Artifact:* each review is stored as a new version of the glob's `independent_review` artifact (label empty; commit SHA = the reviewed commit, the test-merge commit before merge, the squash commit after; provenance `by: reviewer`, actor = the model; `independentReview` in the provenance holds the stage, the PR head at review time and the finding counts). It is indexed for search as the source type `independent_review` (authority: discussion) and shown in the glob view under the review icon. *Card:* the glob view's artifact summaries carry `review` (stage, `headSha`, counts) and the card shows the review icon with "N findings" ("No findings" for none), and "(outdated)" when a pre-merge review was made for a PR head that a later push replaced. *Before merge* (same, super): one GitHub review per reviewed commit, submitted as COMMENT (so it never approves or requests changes) on the PR head, with a summary and inline comments: findings at or above the minimum severity (default `medium`) whose line is in the reviewed diff are inline comments; lower ones and any whose line can't be anchored are listed in the summary body, so none is lost. If GitHub refuses the inline comments (the reviewed change is the test merge, whose lines can differ from the PR's own diff), the review is posted again with the comments folded into its body. The review starts with the marker `<!-- slop-review glob=<id> sha=<reviewed> v=<artifact version> -->`. When a newer review is posted, slop's earlier reviews on the PR (found by the marker's prefix) are marked outdated: their body is rewritten to start "Outdated. A later commit (`<sha>`) was reviewed", and their inline comments are minimized with the OUTDATED classifier (GitHub offers no way to mark a review itself outdated; minimizing is best effort). A neutral check run `slop-review` ("N findings", the counts and the summary) is reported on the PR head; it needs the App's Checks write permission (an installation that hasn't accepted it answers 403 and the check is skipped, the review is still posted), and slop leaves this check out of every reading of a head's checks, so it never makes a head look pending or failed. *After merge* (a sub's merge, or Review code on a merged glob): one PR comment on the merged PR with the whole review, marked with the same marker (a retried job with the same artifact version comments once; a new run comments again). Reviews never block merging. *Settings (as built, s15f43):* an admin sets the board's reviewer under Settings, Automation, "Independent review": on or off (`review.enabled`, default on), the Bedrock model (free text; blank follows `REVIEW_MODEL`, default `global.anthropic.claude-sonnet-5-5` since s15t109, replacing gpt-oss-120b, which reported false findings; Claude models are called with no temperature, other models at 0; a bad ID surfaces as the usual "model unavailable" failure), the minimum severity for inline comments (default `medium`), extra ignored paths (path globs, added to the merge policy's `sizeIgnoredPaths`, which always apply) and the tool cap (1 to 50, blank for the default of 12). They are one `review_settings` column on the board; a missing key reads as its default, so existing boards are unchanged. Turning the reviewer off stops only the automatic reviews (before and after merge, including jobs already queued); "Review code" still reviews on demand. Reviews never block merging either way. A change of only skipped files (lockfiles, generated, ignored) costs no model call and is stored as an empty review. A busy or unavailable model, or an answer that fails its schema twice, fails the attempt and the outbox retries it with its backoff.

**Independent review: feedback (as built, s15f43).** Each inline comment carries a hidden marker `<!-- slop-finding glob=<id> v=<artifact version> f=<fingerprint> -->` (the fingerprint hashes the file, category and title), and its first line is `**severity · category: title**`; both are read back to know which finding a reaction is about. Findings listed only in the review summary have no comment and so take no feedback. Verdicts are stored in `review_feedback` (`wrong`, `thumbs_down` or `resolved`, from a `thread`, a `reply` or a `reaction`; one row per GitHub comment and source, so redeliveries and re-polls add nothing; bots' replies and comments that aren't slop findings, or are on another board's glob, are ignored): (1) *resolved as wrong* is a resolved thread where a person's reply starts with `/wrong` or "false positive" (GitHub has no "wrong" reason on resolving, so the reply is the gesture; the `pull_request_review_thread` webhook records it), or such a reply on its own (`pull_request_review_comment`); (2) a thumbs-down reaction, which GitHub doesn't send a webhook for, is read by a poll that runs just before each board's weekly mining (`GET /pulls/{n}/comments` for the PRs of globs reviewed in the mining window; best effort, errors logged, mining runs regardless), which also reads `/wrong` replies the webhook missed. The mining input (`BoardActivity.reviewFeedback`) carries the feedback created in the window, and the `review_false_positive` signal counts `wrong` and `thumbs_down` only. Reactions are therefore at most a week late.

- **Auto-fix:** routine PRs (subs, sames) use Claude's auto-fix: the routine's cloud session watches the PR and pushes fixes for CI failures and review comments, asking first when ambiguous. It uses the routine owner's subscription. Supers rely on the developer or an `@claude` mention, which needs the Claude GitHub App and a Claude workflow (catalog `claude.yml`); readiness checks the workflow.
- **Routines on sames:** the routine works on the glob's branch (e.g. `s1t4`), and a developer who picks the glob up works in the same branch, branching off it only if ever needed. Pick-up is refused while a routine run is active; Take over supersedes the run. After a human picks up, the routine's auto-fix stands down.
- **Release review:** not planned for now.

## Environments and deployment

**The model (s15t262).** Board environments are the **dev environments only**: the branch-deploy targets (for example dev, danzig, demo), each with its deploy project. There are no environment roles, no production flag, no "deploys are known by" setting, no default environment for subs and no base-branch setting. Slop deploys a glob's branch to a dev environment and nothing else: it never tracks int, staging or prod deploys, never learns which glob a release contains, and keeps no containment check, environment deploy record or "Mark deployed" action for them. A person ticks the step for the rest of the pipeline (see Downstream steps below).

- **Base branch:** the repo's default branch, read from GitHub, cached and refreshed on a push or when settings change. It is not a board setting. Wherever this document says master or main, it means that branch.
- **Steps run on the glob's dev environment, or on the base** when the glob has none: an `after_deploy:<dev env>` step falls due when the glob's branch is deployed there, which can be before the merge; an after-merge step needs the base to have the change; a once step needs neither.
- **Downstream steps:** a step may carry the `downstream` flag (a rollout step's `(downstream)` or `(downstream: int, staging, prod)` option; `downstream` on `add_human_step`) when its command must also run as the change is released further. Slop then adds one more step, "Run on <the names, or the rest of the pipeline> as each is released: <title>", worded from the board's environments knowledge doc (it lists the downstream names). That step **blocks nothing** (not sign-off, not dependants, not merge), **keeps the Arm system label** while open, also after sign-off (the glob sits in Signed off with Arm system showing, and is not archived, until it is ticked), and is ticked by a person. Steps that named int, staging or prod were converted into a downstream step on their glob at migration.
- **Build and deploy events** come from CodeBuild and CodePipeline through EventBridge. An EventBridge API destination posts them to slop's webhook endpoint with an API key header, where a driving adapter translates them into domain commands such as `BuildCompleted`, and the core never sees EventBridge.
- **Build and ATF results:** branch builds attach to the glob named by the branch. ATF runs after the change is merged to main and deployed to int, started by the int pipeline (slop does not start it on a dev branch deploy and does not track int deploys); a run that names a dev deploy's commit attaches to the glob(s) of that deploy. Details are fetched on demand through a `TestResultSource` port backed by the CodeBuild reports API.
- **Hotfixes** (deferred, not part of the first build) are `h` globs with their own counter, worked on a branch off a release branch; the commit message uses the hotfix ID (e.g. `s1h3: backport`). Slop does not track where they are released.

**ATF failures:** any failing test counts as a failure. A failure is flagged clearly on the card but never blocks PR creation, PR approval, sign-off, merge or a release, including ATF runs against feature branches.

**Environment assignment for product work:** each board has an enumerated list of dev (branch-deploy) environments, each with its deploy project; a new board starts with a single environment, main (enough for slop's own development). **Each new code glob gets a dev environment automatically** by one process: a free one first; when all are busy, the one whose holder is furthest along, then the least recently deployed. That is the only automatic route; there is no board default for subs. A glob's environment can also be chosen at creation (MCP input, board form, `slop new --env` or `sstor --new --env`) or at pick-up (`pick_up`, the REST pick-up and take-over actions, `slop pick-up --env` or `sstor --glob --env`; a pick-up without one keeps the glob's environment) and edited in the glob view at any time; an explicit choice wins, and intake suggests an environment the request names. **Slop starts the deploy:** when a push to a glob branch arrives through the GitHub webhook and the glob has an environment set, slop calls CodeBuild's `StartBuild` (through a `Deployer` port, using its IAM role) with the branch and target environment as parameters. If none is set, nothing deploys. The core rejects assigning a glob to something that isn't a board environment. Deploys run one at a time per environment: each is pinned to an exact commit SHA (StartBuild with that SHA, never a moving branch), and while one is running only the newest pending request for that environment is kept. The environment's live state is recorded from the deploy that actually succeeded, so the board always reflects what is running. A changed environment applies on the next push, or immediately with a *Deploy now* button. Slop records which glob is live in each environment, so the card shows when another glob's deploy has replaced it in a shared environment. Merged work reaches int, staging and prod through the repo's own pipeline, which slop does not observe. Slop's GitHub App also puts the glob's type and environment on its PR as labels (e.g. `slop:sub`, `env:dev`) for the repo's build scripts, which decide which checks and tests run.

**Branch deploys (as built):**

- **Records, not statuses:** each request is a deploy record (`waiting`, `running`, `succeeded`, `failed`, `replaced`) in its own table; deploy state is a flag beside the glob's status and never a transition. Per environment at most one runs and one waits; a new request replaces the waiting one (`replaced`), and when the running one finishes the waiting one starts. The environment's live glob is the one whose deploy last succeeded there.
- **Triggers:** a push to a glob with an environment, in Doing, records a `request_deploy` outbox effect in the push's transaction (not for a superseded run's push or the `<id>: start` commit); the executor queues it, ignoring a repeat of the same pushed commit. *Deploy now* deploys the PR head and is open to any board member, disabled for everyone while a deploy runs in that environment.
- **Providers** sit behind a `Deployer` port and run the repo's `.sstor/deploy.sh <env>` with `SLOP_ENVIRONMENT`, `SLOP_GLOB`, `SLOP_SHA`, `SLOP_DEPLOY_ID` and `SLOP_CALLBACK_URL`. CodeBuild: `StartBuild` on the environment's project (or the board default) with the commit as source version. GitHub Actions: planned (`workflow_dispatch` plus `workflow_run` events). A deploy that can't start, or whose glob was deleted, is recorded as failed so the queue moves on.
- **Results:** CodeBuild state changes through EventBridge's API destination to `POST /webhooks/aws` (key header, one of the keys in `AWS_WEBHOOK_KEY`, since each deploy target generates its own; builds slop didn't start are ignored), and a signed per-deploy callback `POST /webhooks/deploy/{id}` that `deploy.sh` may call with `succeeded` or `failed`. Repeated results are ignored.
- **Deploy targets** (`infra`, `DeployTargetStack`): either a new CodeBuild project that runs the repo's `.sstor/deploy.sh`, or existing deploy projects whose own buildspec deploys; for those only the EventBridge rule is added, so their results come through `/webhooks/aws` alone.
- **Board:** cards show deploying or queued, live, deploy failed (with the reason, until the next success) or replaced by another glob; the glob view has Deploy now and the deploy history; Settings (Environments and deploys) holds the dev environments and the integration (CodeBuild region and each environment's own deploy project, or a GitHub Actions workflow).

**Integration and release deploys (removed, s15t262).** Slop no longer observes int, staging or release deploys: the `slop.ci` "Slop Environment Deployed" event, `report-deploy.sh`, `environment_deploys`, `glob_environments`, the `check_environment` containment effect, environment roles, the production flag, the stall for those deploys, "Record a deploy" and `mark_environment_deployed` for them are gone, and board 14's int, staging and prod environments were dropped at migration. What a person must do as the change is released is a downstream step (see above). Branch deploys and their history are unchanged. Slop does not start ATF on a dev branch deploy either: ATF runs after the merge to main is deployed to int, started by the int pipeline (the pipeline still reads the spec list and posts results through the ATF endpoints below).

**Review guide (R3, as built):**
- **Review guide:** a board knowledge document with area `review_guide` (catalog `review_guide_template`), served by `get_review_guide(repo)`.

**ATF results (as built):**

- **Slop only observes ATF.** The board's pipeline runs it (the catalog's `buildspec-atf.yml`, a stage after a deploy, running the repo's `.sstor/atf.sh` and keeping its JUnit XML as a CodeBuild report group) and then `.sstor/atf-report.sh`, whatever the result. That counts the JUnit test cases (passed, failed or errored, skipped) and puts a `slop.ci` "Slop ATF Completed" event on the default bus (detail `repo`, `passed`, `failed`, optional `skipped`, `sha`, `url`, and an `environment` or a `branch`, or both). It reaches `POST /webhooks/aws` the same way as deploy events, and each board records it once per event ID (`test_runs`). Slop does not start ATF itself: it runs after the change is merged to main and deployed to int, started by the int pipeline (e.g. the int megarepo builder). EventBridge is the AWS-only way in; any runner can instead post the same report over HTTP with its own credential (*ATF runners*, below).
- **Branch runs:** a `branch` that is a glob's ID (or `refs/heads/<id>`) makes the run that glob's, on its own board only; it needs the commit it tested and logs `ATFCompleted` on the glob. It shows as stale when that commit is no longer the PR's head, and it never drives a transition.
- **Environment runs:** otherwise the run belongs to a dev environment's branch deploy: the `sha` it reports, or the environment's latest succeeded deploy when it arrives without one. It is recorded once per board, not per glob (no events), and shows on the glob(s) of that deploy. A run for an older commit that arrives after a newer deploy is kept but shows on no glob.
- **Board:** a failure is any failing test, flagged only. The glob view shows the failing runs (`ATF n failed` in red when a current run failed; muted when the only run is stale), and the card shows none (s15b160); each run links to its report. The glob view's Tests section lists the branch runs and the environment runs that apply. With several runners the glob view still lists each run (see *ATF runners*). Results live beside the glob: a branch run sends a `glob.deploys` hint, and an environment run sends one `board.tests` hint for the whole board.
- **Spec list for a deploy (s15f163):** the pipeline asks slop which specs to run. *Pull (default):* `GET /api/boards/{b}/atf-specs?environment=&sha=` (board members) or `GET /integrations/boards/{b}/atf-specs` (a runner's own credential as bearer, bound to that board and to the environments the runner serves; the board's integration token still works, deprecated: see *ATF runners*). `environment` is 1-100 characters and `sha` 7-40 hex, else 422; a non-member gets 403, a refused credential 401 (plain, whatever the reason), an environment the runner doesn't serve 403, an environment that isn't one of the board's dev environments 422. Answers: `202 {status:'pending', retryAfterSeconds:15}` while the branch deploy has not succeeded yet; `409 {status:'superseded', latestSha}` when the sha is an older deploy; else `200 {status:'ready', environment, sha, specs:[{spec, globs}], greps:[{grep, globs}], batchId}`. The list is the latest test plan's `atf-specs` of the glob(s) of that deploy (the branch deploy's own glob; a deploy has one string id, and the deploy-live webhook carries it as `deployId`). The list is recomputed from those test plans on every call (nothing is stored, so a plan edited between two calls can change the answer). An empty union means the board's default suite. Several greps are combined as `(?:a)|(?:b)`. *Scripts:* the catalog's `atf-specs.sh` fetches the list (retrying on 202), exports `SLOP_ATF_SPECS` (newline-separated) and `SLOP_ATF_GREP`, and every failure is a warning with the default suite; the board's `.sstor/atf.sh` runs only those specs when the list is set. *Push (per-board option):* a board whose `atf` setting is `{provider:'codebuild', region, project, roleArn?, mode:'push'}` would get a `start_atf` outbox effect (the executor stays, but a dev branch deploy no longer queues it; nothing queues it today): it starts the project with `SLOP_ATF_SPECS`, `SLOP_ATF_GREP`, `SLOP_ENVIRONMENT` and `SLOP_SHA` overrides and an idempotency token per board, environment and commit; the AWS side (project, role, trust) is a person's rollout step, never slop's. *Push is restricted by the server (s15b125):* `ATF_PUSH_ALLOWED_PROJECTS` (exact names or `prefix*`) and `ATF_PUSH_ALLOWED_ROLE_ACCOUNTS` decide which project and role a board may name; with no project allow-list push mode is off (no `StartBuild` with slop's own credentials for a board-chosen project). A setting outside them is refused with a plain error when saved and again when the start runs (the effect is dropped and logged). Only a server admin (`SLOP_SERVER_ADMINS`) can turn push on or change its project or role (`pushChangedBy`, `pushChangedAt` are recorded; a board admin may switch to pull or leave a push setup as is). `AssumeRole` always sends `ExternalId`, a per-board value slop generates and stores in the `atf` setting on the first push role and keeps when the role changes; the role's trust policy requires it as an `sts:ExternalId` condition (catalog README).
- **Per-spec results (s15f163):** `atf-report.sh` also sends `specs:[{file, status, tests, failed}]` (status passed, failed, skipped or missing; JUnit `file`, then classname, then suite name; at most 1000 rows) stored in `test_run_specs`; slop adds `missing` for batch specs a run did not report. A glob's planned specs take the newest result among its own branch runs and the runs at commits that hold it; the glob view's run line counts those when the run reported them (red only for the glob's own failures, amber for missing), and its Test plan tab and Tests section list each spec with its result and run link, or no result yet. Unplanned results attach to the environment's commit as before. Policy is unchanged: a failure is flagged and never blocks.
- **ATF runners (s15f177):** a board registers each system that runs its ATF as a *runner* (`atf_runners`: name, method, `config`, the dev environments it serves, enabled, revoked, last seen, last result), so the single shared board token is no longer the way in. Board admins manage them under `/api/boards/{b}/atf-runners` (`GET` list, `POST` register, `PATCH {id}` rename, enable or disable, change environments or settings, `DELETE {id}` revoke); a member who isn't an admin gets 403. A method is `oidc` (`config.issuer` an https URL and `config.claims`, claim name to values, each value exact or `prefix*`), `aws_iam` (`config.roleArns`, optionally `config.accounts`) or `client_credentials` (`config.clientId`, the Cognito client slop created for it); the registry only holds who may present a credential, never a credential's secret. Any method may also carry `config.webhook` (`{url}`), which subscribes the runner to the deploy-live webhook (see *ATF runners: deploy-live webhook and the `RunnerStarter` port*); its sealed signing secret is the one secret a runner's config holds, and no answer shows it. Rules: names are unique per board among runners that aren't revoked (compared without regard to case); at least one environment, each one a board dev environment; an `oidc` runner needs at least one claim rule, and one whose issuer is GitHub Actions' (`token.actions.githubusercontent.com`) must pin `repository` or `sub`, because any workflow on GitHub can get a token for any audience; a rule can't be `*` alone. Revoking a runner frees its name and ends its credential for good; its runs stay and keep pointing at it, and no other runner and not the Meet notes token is affected. Which identity methods verify credentials is built one per part: this change adds the registry, the resolver and the legacy token; a runner whose method has no verifier yet simply can't authenticate. *The resolver* (`atf-auth.ts`) turns a bearer credential and a board into a runner of that board or nothing: each method is a verifier in one list; the runner a verifier names is re-read and must be enabled, not revoked and on that board; two different runners from the verifiers is an ambiguity and is refused; only when no verifier answers is the credential tried as the board's integration token. Every refusal is a plain 401 with no detail, and tokens, secrets and claims are never logged (a runner's ID and method at most). *The legacy token (deprecated for ATF):* accepted on `atf-specs` and `atf-results`, resolves to no registered runner (`runner_id` null, shown as "Legacy runner"), every answer carries `Deprecation: true` and a `Link` to the catalog README, and its use is logged once an hour per board; it stays valid for the Meet notes inbox, which accepts no runner credential. *Results over HTTP:* `POST /integrations/boards/{b}/atf-results` takes the "Slop ATF Completed" detail as the body (`passed`, `failed`, `skipped`, `sha`, `url`, `specs`, and an `environment` or a `branch`; `repo` is optional and ignored because the credential fixes the board; `at` is when it finished), at most 256 KB (413). A bad shape is 422; an environment outside the runner's list 403; an environment that isn't a board dev environment, a branch of another board's glob and a redelivery are `200 {status:'ignored'}`, a recorded run `201 {status:'recorded'}`. The run is recorded on the credential's board only, with `test_runs.runner_id`, and stamps the runner's last result. A redelivery is recorded once through the event ID `runner:<id>:<key>` (`legacy` for the token), where the key is the `Idempotency-Key` header (1 to 200 characters) or else a hash of the runner, commit, environment, branch, counts and `at`; so two identical reports with no key and no `at` count as one delivery. *Several runners at one commit:* each runner's newest run counts (per runner and environment on a card, per runner for a branch). For a planned spec a failed row from any runner wins, then passed, skipped and missing, and a spec is `missing` only when no runner has another row for it, so one runner's derived `missing` rows never hide another runner's result; the card's indicators of one group (the current branch runs, or one environment's runs) share the combined planned-spec counts, so the chip is red when any runner failed a planned spec and amber when a spec is missing from all. Runs without a runner (the legacy token, EventBridge) count as one more runner. Runs and indicators carry `runnerName` only when the board has more than one runner (registered, or among the runs shown).
- **ATF runner identity: OIDC and AWS IAM (s15f177):** `oidc` and `aws_iam` runners authenticate with a JWT, checked by one verifier (`atf-oidc.ts`, `aws-jwt-verify`, RS and ES algorithms). The server trusts only the issuers it lists: `ATF_OIDC_ALLOWED_ISSUERS` for `oidc` runners and `ATF_AWS_ALLOWED_ISSUERS` (an AWS account's outbound-identity-federation issuer) for `aws_iam` runners, exact https URLs, no issuer on both lists, both off while empty. The token's `iss` only picks which listed issuer to verify against; discovery (`/.well-known/openid-configuration`, whose `issuer` must match and whose `jwks_uri` must stay on the issuer's origin) and JWKS fetches have a 5 s timeout, a 256 KB cap, no redirects and a cache. The audience must be `ATF_OIDC_AUDIENCE` (default the public URL); the signature, expiry (required) and `alg` (never `none`) must verify. An `oidc` runner then matches when the token's issuer is the runner's and every claim rule matches; an `aws_iam` runner matches when the token's `sub` (a role ARN, or an assumed-role ARN of that role) is one of its `roleArns` and the account is in `ATF_AWS_ALLOWED_ACCOUNTS` (a setting separate from the push one) and agrees with the token's `aws_account` claim. A token that matches zero or two or more enabled runners on the board is a plain 401. The catalog scripts pick the identity from the environment (a CI token, else AWS credentials when no board token is set, else the board token) and post results over HTTP with a per-pipeline-run `Idempotency-Key`; EventBridge stays when selected. `catalog/scripts/README.md` is the runner contract and the registration steps.
- **ATF runner identity: client credentials (s15f177):** a runner with neither OIDC nor AWS identity gets its own Cognito app client in slop's user pool. The pool's `slop` resource server has the scopes `atf.read-specs` and `atf.write-results` (`slop/atf.read-specs`, `slop/atf.write-results`; `infra/lib/auth-stack.ts`), and no client of the auth stack carries them. *Lifecycle:* when a board admin registers a `client_credentials` runner (`POST /api/boards/{b}/atf-runners`) and the server has the user pool settings (`AUTH_MODE=cognito`), slop saves the runner disabled (holding the name), creates the client through the `RunnerCredentialIssuer` port (adapter `atf-cognito.ts` on `@aws-sdk/client-cognito-identity-provider`: a secret, the `client_credentials` flow only, both scopes, one-hour access tokens, named `slop-atf-b<board>-r<runner>`), stores only `config.clientId` and enables it. A `clientId` the caller sends is ignored and the stored one never changes. The `201` answer carries `credentials: {clientId, clientSecret, tokenUrl, scope}` once, with `Cache-Control: no-store`; the secret is never stored, listed again or logged, and a lost one means revoking and registering again. When Cognito fails the runner is revoked at once (name freed, never usable) and the call is a 422 that saves nothing. `DELETE {id}` marks the runner revoked first and then deletes the client (a client already gone is success); when Cognito fails the answer is still 200 with a `warning`, the runner is unusable anyway, and revoking again retries the deletion. Without the pool settings a `client_credentials` runner can be registered with a `clientId` but can't authenticate. *Verification* (`createCognitoRunnerVerifier`, `aws-jwt-verify`, `tokenUse: 'access'` for the pool, no fixed client): the token must verify against the pool, carry no `username` (a person's token never counts), carry the scope the door needs (`slop/atf.read-specs` for `atf-specs`, `slop/atf.write-results` for `atf-results`; a door that names none grants nothing) and a `client_id` that names exactly one enabled, unrevoked `client_credentials` runner of the board. The resolver passes the door's scope to every verifier (OIDC and AWS tokens carry none and ignore it); every refusal is a plain 401. The user verifier in `auth.ts` is unchanged and never accepts such a token, because a runner's client is not among `COGNITO_CLIENT_IDS`. *Capability:* `/api/health` reports `atfClientCredentials`, on only while a `DescribeResourceServer` probe (cached a minute) finds both scopes and the instance role may call Cognito for the pool; the reason is in `capabilityReasons`. The instance role gets `cognito-idp:Create/Delete/DescribeUserPoolClient` and `DescribeResourceServer` on that pool's ARN only (`-c cognitoUserPoolId` on the host stack). Cognito charges per successful token request (about $2.25 per 1,000 at the first tier at the time of writing; confirm on the pricing page), so a script should reuse its token for its hour.
- **ATF runners: deploy-live webhook and the `RunnerStarter` port (s15f177):** pull stays the default and push is only for runners that can't pull. *Push behind a port:* `RunnerStarter` (core, `start(job)` with the job the push start always had: board, `atf` integration, environment, commit, specs, greps) is what the `start_atf` effect calls; `CodeBuildAtfStarter` (`atf-starter.ts`) is its one adapter, unchanged: the same server allow-lists (`ATF_PUSH_ALLOWED_PROJECTS`, `ATF_PUSH_ALLOWED_ROLE_ACCOUNTS`), the same refusals (`AtfRefusedError`, now core's class, so any adapter can raise it) and the same idempotency token. A vendor adapter is added only when a board needs one. *Deploy-live webhook:* a runner whose `config.webhook` is `{url}` (any method) would be told when a deploy is live. A succeeded dev branch deploy no longer queues `deliver_atf_webhook` (ATF starts from the int pipeline after the merge, not from a dev deploy); the executor and the one-job-per-runner-and-deploy dedupe stay for when a queuer is added: the store keeps one job per runner and deploy in any state, so a repeated check, a retry that already succeeded or a second queueing never delivers twice, and a delivery that was dropped (the receiver refused it) is not queued again with the same delivery ID. *Registration:* the URL must be https, with no user name or password, a public host name (not `localhost`, a single label or an internal-looking suffix), and any literal IP address must be public (private, loopback, link-local, multicast, documentation and other reserved IPv4 and IPv6 ranges are refused, mapped IPv4 inside IPv6 included); the server also resolves the host when the webhook is set and refuses one whose addresses are not all public. *The signing secret is slop's:* when a webhook is turned on slop makes a `whsec_` secret and returns it once as `webhookSecret` on the `POST` (and on the `PATCH` that turns the webhook on, both `Cache-Control: no-store`); a `secret` sent by a caller is refused. An HMAC needs the secret itself, so it is kept in the runner's `config.webhook.secret` sealed with AES-256-GCM under a key derived from `SIGNING_SECRET` (the existing server secret; slop's Secrets Manager entries are for the server's own few secrets, a per-runner entry would need new IAM and cost per secret) and removed from every API answer; moving the URL keeps the secret, turning the webhook off and on again makes a new one, and changing `SIGNING_SECRET` makes the sealed secrets unreadable (deliveries are dropped and logged until the webhook is turned off and on again). Without `SIGNING_SECRET` (local development) webhooks can't be set. *Delivery:* the executor re-reads the runner and the deploy and drops the effect when the runner is gone, off or no longer serves the environment, its webhook was removed, a newer deploy replaced the commit, or the URL now resolves to a private address. It resolves the host once, requires every address to be public and connects to the first, with the host name (and a port other than 443) as `Host`, and the name as SNI, so the certificate is checked against the name (no second lookup, so DNS rebinding can't redirect it); no redirect is followed, the exchange has a 10 s deadline, the answer's body is never kept (at most 64 KiB is read) and the request body is capped. It POSTs JSON `{event:'deploy.live', id, board:{id,name}, environment, sha, deployId, deployedAt}` with headers `Slop-Event`, `Slop-Delivery` (`atf-<runner>-<deploy>`, the same on every retry so a receiver can ignore a repeat), `Slop-Timestamp` (Unix seconds) and `Slop-Signature: sha256=<hex>`, the HMAC-SHA256 of `<timestamp>.<body>` under the runner's secret; a receiver should check the signature in constant time and reject an old timestamp. A 2xx ends it; a network failure, a timeout, a 408, a 429 or a 5xx is retried by the outbox with its usual backoff and attempts; any other answer (a redirect, a 4xx) is the receiver's refusal and ends it, logged. Failures never block a deploy or a merge, and nothing logs the URL, the secret, the signature or the body (runner and board IDs only). EventBridge delivery is unchanged. *Not built:* the signed-webhook identity method (a vendor posting results with its own HMAC) and vendor push adapters.
- **Deferred:** the `TestResultSource` port (the CodeBuild reports API). Counts arrive in the event and the link opens the report, which covers the card and the glob view; the port comes with `get_test_results`, which needs the failing tests' names. Posting a failure summary on the PR is deferred with it.


## Time tracking and reports

Time counts only during working hours, and only toward each person's single active glob.

- **Event log:** an append-only record of every board event (moved, assigned, picked up, PR opened, merged, label changed), stored as rows per glob. Reports and aging are derived from it.
- **Active glob:** the one the person most recently moved into Doing or picked up; one active glob per person across all boards. Counting for a glob stops when it leaves Doing or another glob becomes the person's active one; an older glob does not resume automatically, only when it is re-entered or picked up again. Example: Ana picks up s1t4 at 10:00 and s1b2 at 14:00, so s1t4 gets 4 hours and s1b2 counts from 14:00. Example: a glob entered Doing on Friday at 16:00 and merged on Monday at 10:00 counts 2 hours (Friday 16:00–17:00 and Monday 09:00–10:00).
- **Working hours:** 9:00–17:00 in one configurable time zone for everyone, defaulting to UTC and shown on the board. Servers often run in UTC, so the time zone is a setting rather than the server's. Weekends are excluded; holidays are ignored.
- **Attribution:** the planner until a human implementer picks the glob up, then the implementer.
- **Reports:** monthly and yearly % RnD (R&D work kind) vs maintenance (the rest) per developer, per board. Each report is built whole from the event log when it is asked for.

**Report output:** a CSV the board offers as a download, computed when downloaded. Columns: developer, period, RnD hours, maintenance hours, % RnD.

**As built (slice 10)**

- **Calculation:** pure, in core (`packages/core/src/domain/time-tracking.ts`). Nothing is recorded as it happens: each build replays every board's `GlobCreated`, `StatusChanged`, `PickedUp` and `FieldsChanged` events in log order, keeping each person's single active glob and each glob's status, category, planner and implementer. A glob counts for a person only while it is their active glob, it is in Doing (implementing, in_progress, failed, pr_open, merging), and it is attributed to them: the implementer, else the planner.
- **Humans only:** a glob becomes a person's active glob when they move it into Doing themselves (a `StatusChanged` into Doing with them as actor: Start, Start now, a same becoming a sub) or pick it up (`PickedUp`, including take over), and when they create a super straight into Doing (they are its implementer). A glob created straight into Doing (a sub, an auto-started same) and system moves (null actor: a release, a revert, an observed merge) change nobody's active glob, so a routine starting a sub doesn't stop the planner's count; a sub's time counts for nobody unless its planner moves it into Doing or picks it up. Moving someone else's glob into Doing makes it your active glob (your previous one stops) but counts nothing unless you are attributed.
- **Stopping:** leaving Doing, or the glob's attribution moving to someone else (a pick-up by another person; every move to implementing and a same's start again clear the implementer), clears it as the active glob of whoever had it, so an older glob never resumes by itself, only when re-entered or picked up again. A work kind change (`FieldsChanged`, or the re-inference after a plan change) splits the time: hours count in the work kind the glob had when they were worked (old events without a work kind read it from their category).
- **Working hours:** 09:00–17:00 on weekdays in `SLOP_WORK_TIME_ZONE` (an IANA name, default `UTC`, checked when the server starts; one zone for everyone, shown read-only in Settings, Reports). Each local day's window is converted on its own, so DST changes are handled, and a day's hours belong to its local date, which decides the month and year. Holidays are ignored.
- **Reports:** a period is a month (`2026-10`) or a year (`2026`) in the work time zone. One CSV row per developer (their email) with hours, sorted by developer; developers with no hours are left out, and an empty period is a header-only CSV. Header `developer,period,RnD hours,maintenance hours,% RnD`; hours to 2 decimals, % RnD to 1 decimal from the unrounded totals; RFC 4180 quoting with CRLF line ends, and a value starting with `=`, `+`, `-`, `@`, a tab or a carriage return gets a leading `'`. R&D versus maintenance is the glob's work kind (subs included); old events fall back to feature = R&D, task and bug = maintenance. A glob still in Doing counts up to the time the report is computed.
- **Computed on demand, not stored:** the original design had a scheduled batch job writing each report to S3. As built there is no job and nothing is written to S3 or Postgres. Each download (`ReportService.report`) reads every board's events of those four types up to the period's end, or up to now for the running period, and builds the report whole, so it always reflects the current log. A period that hasn't started is refused. Why: the log is small and the calculation is quick, so a stored copy would only add a job, a table and stale snapshots.
- **Read cost:** the store filters the event types in SQL and returns only the `data` keys the replay reads (`status`, `to`, `category`, `type`), so whole payloads (a glob's creation record, a plan diff) stay in the database. The globs those events name are read the same way (`Tx.globFacts`: id, board, planner, category and type only), with the IDs sent as one JSON parameter so a long log can't exceed Postgres's bind-parameter limit. There is no index on `events (type, at)`: the query is a sequential scan of `events`, which at slop's size takes milliseconds, and a report for the running period needs nearly the whole table anyway. An index would mostly help reports for old periods; add one (a migration) before caching if downloads become slow.
- **Per board, with a cross-board replay:** a report belongs to one board and counts only time on that board's globs. The replay still runs over every board's events, because a person has one active glob across all boards: starting a glob on board B stops their count on board A even though B's hours don't appear in A's report. Rows are therefore the board's own hours per developer, and the same person can appear in several boards' reports with different hours.
- **Access:** a board's admins download its reports (checked before the period, so anyone else gets the same refusal for any period); any member of the board sees the time zone. The Reports group of the board's Settings page offers the current month, the 12 before it, and the current and previous year; each is fetched as JSON and saved from the browser, so a refusal shows as a message rather than a saved file. `slop report [--board <n>] --period <YYYY-MM|YYYY> [--out <path>|-]` (the board defaults to `SLOP_BOARD`) writes `slop-report-<board>-<period>.csv` in the current folder (`--out -` prints it), after checking the board and period slop returns; the `get_report` MCP tool (board, period) returns it with the CSV.
- **Known limits:** deleting a glob deletes its events, so its hours leave the reports (and a glob its person had left for it may appear to count on until it leaves Doing). Because reports are computed each time, a past period's numbers change if the log does (a deletion, a category edit). Events carry no routine marker: a routine calling `start_glob` or `pick_up` acts as its owner and counts as the owner's move. Start again of a super resets its implementer to the creator without an event, so a pick-up by someone else keeps counting for them until the next pick-up.

## Data, auth and infrastructure

Slop is one portable Docker image running as a single instance, backed by Postgres. AWS-specific services sit behind adapters, so the image can move to any host; sign-in is the one remaining AWS tie.

| Concern | Choice |
| --- | --- |
| Hosting | One container (Node with Hono or Fastify) on a small EC2 instance with an IAM instance role, run with Docker Compose alongside Postgres. The same Compose file runs locally. |
| Frontend | Vite + React + TypeScript, Tailwind + shadcn/ui, dnd-kit, served by the same container. The board is built from scratch rather than adapting an open-source kanban app; community shadcn-based kanban components can serve as a starting point |
| API | REST, MCP, webhooks and live updates all served by the one server |
| Database | PostgreSQL with full-text search and pgvector, holding globs, events, communications and search. Runs in Docker (official Postgres image with pgvector) on the same EC2 instance. Aurora Serverless v2 is the managed alternative if point-in-time restore is ever needed. |
| Migrations | Drizzle, run on deploy |
| Live updates | Server-sent events from an in-process notifier |
| Artifacts | S3-compatible storage with bucket versioning, served via presigned URLs |
| LLM and embeddings | Amazon Bedrock behind the LLM port (Claude Haiku 4.5 for intake, Opus 5.5 for the KB pipeline); a direct Anthropic API adapter is possible |
| Sign-in | Cognito federated to IAM Identity Center |
| GitHub | GitHub App with installation tokens; source stays on GitHub |
| CodeBuild events | EventBridge API destination posting to slop's webhook with an API key header |
| Secrets | Environment variables for configuration; Secrets Manager for sensitive credentials (GitHub App key, routine fire tokens), read via the EC2 instance role: with `SECRETS=aws` the server reads `slop/prod/{signing-secret,cognito-board-client-secret,slack-signing-secret,slack-bot-token,github-app}` at start (a missing required one stops it, naming the secret) and routines on demand, cached for `SECRETS_CACHE_SECONDS` (30) |
| Infrastructure | CDK for the AWS resources (Cognito, S3, IAM, EventBridge, host) |

**Auth**

- **Board:** the server handles the Cognito login (federated to Identity Center) and keeps a server-side session cookie (30 days). The OAuth `state` is signed by the server and bound to the browser by a nonce cookie, which stops login CSRF. A plain-http `localhost` dev server can accept the signed state without the cookie, because Chrome drops it on the cross-site return from Cognito; that exception is off unless the server is started with `LOCAL_SIGN_IN_WITHOUT_COOKIE=true` (`scripts/dev.sh` sets it), so a proxy rewriting `Host` to `localhost` can't open login CSRF on a deployed slop.
- **Claude app connector and routines:** Cognito is the OAuth authorization server, using a pre-registered app client whose ID and secret go in the connector's Advanced settings, with callback `https://claude.ai/api/mcp/auth_callback`. Routines use the account's connector.
- **Claude Code:** OAuth login against Cognito via `claude mcp add --client-id`, once in the browser, then automatic refresh.
- **Sessionator (the slop CLI):** the same public client as Claude Code, with its own callback (`localhost:7780`): `sstor login` runs the authorization code flow with PKCE once in the browser, keeps the tokens in the macOS Keychain and refreshes automatically. sstor calls slop's `/mcp` directly with the access token; it never starts a Claude session to reach slop.
- **Slop verifies Cognito JWTs** with `aws-jwt-verify`, checking issuer and client ID, and publishes the protected-resource discovery document pointing clients at Cognito.
- **Integrations** use per-integration secrets, not user sign-in: a per-user ingest secret for the Apps Script (it also identifies the user), the Slack app's signing secret, the GitHub App webhook secret, and an API key on the EventBridge connection. **Public endpoints:** only the script catalog is served without authentication; it is board-agnostic and contains nothing sensitive or project-specific. The agent set is board knowledge, fetched through the authenticated MCP. Everything specific to a board, including its knowledge, settings and review guide, stays behind OAuth. There is no board token.
- **Roles live in slop.** Identity Center only answers who someone is; email is the stable user ID, with Cognito's user ID and the GitHub username stored against it.
- **Offboarding:** removing someone from Identity Center blocks new logins; a deactivate action in slop rejects their requests immediately.
- **Cognito plan:** Lite. Federated users are free up to 50 monthly active users.
- **Local development:** a dev Cognito pool in CDK with native test users; agents get real tokens with `aws cognito-idp initiate-auth`, and slop validates them exactly as in production.
- **Fallback:** if the connector checks fail, slop becomes its own authorization server (MCP SDK helpers, with dynamic client registration) and Cognito handles login only; Keycloak in the Compose file is the alternative.

**Token lifetimes:** access tokens last one hour and refresh silently; the Cognito app clients for the Claude connector and Claude Code use the maximum refresh token lifetime (10 years), so each person logs in once. Every MCP request is still authenticated. A leaked refresh token is handled by slop's deactivate-user action (slop rejects deactivated users regardless of token) and by revoking the person's tokens in Cognito.

**Search**

- **Communications** (meeting notes, chat threads) are an entity: metadata and text in Postgres, raw files in S3, linked to any number of globs.
- **Ingest** uses one canonical format, `IngestCommunication { source, sourceId, author, occurredAt, title, text, participants }`, on a generic endpoint authenticated by the per-user ingest secret. Scripts (Apps Script, exporters) send that format; only sources with fixed formats, such as the Slack app, get a dedicated adapter. Repeats are ignored by `sourceId`.
- **Indexed content:** communications, glob summaries and plans, implementation records and KB entries, with board, glob, source, person and date as metadata. Search is per board.
- **Keyword** search uses Postgres full-text search; **semantic** search uses pgvector with Bedrock embeddings. Both sit behind the `SearchIndex` port and update when data is saved.
- **One chat-style input on the board:** the question goes to Claude via Bedrock with `search_text`, `search_semantic` and `get_glob` tools scoped to the board; Claude chooses the searches and answers with links to the globs and meetings it used. Responses stream.

**Not chosen:** Lambda, DynamoDB, AppSync, Amplify Gen 2 backend tooling, Bedrock Knowledge Bases on S3 Vectors (semantic search only), OpenSearch Serverless (minimum cost), Supabase, Firestore, PocketBase, Convex, Keycloak (kept as fallback), Aurora Serverless v2 and Neon (Postgres on the VM chosen), Fargate, Lightsail (no instance IAM roles), Google Workspace sign-in, CodeCommit, and App Runner (closed to new customers since 30 April 2026).

## Board notifications

One bar across the top of every board page shows board-wide incidents that need a person. It is not per-glob status. Board setup (readiness) is on it too, quietly: its severity follows the item's state, so setup that can wait for weeks never sounds like an incident.

- **Model.** A `BoardNotification` has an `id` (`<board or all>/<source>`), `boardId` (null for every board), `source` (`main-red`, `integration:<id>`, `local-follow`, `readiness`, `readiness:<item>`), `severity` (`critical`, `warning`, `info`), `title`, `detail`, `link`, an optional `action` (label and href), `since`, and how it clears: `condition` (its source clears it), `until` (a time) or `dismissible` (for everyone) or `personal` (for the person who dismisses it). A `personal` one lists the `items` it is about and, per person, the items they had seen when they dismissed it. There is one per board and source; raising again replaces it and keeps its `since`.
- **Storage.** Beside the board, with no glob or board version. Sources raise and clear through the core `NotificationSink` port, implemented by `NotificationService`, which sends a `board.notifications` hint on every change. Expired `until` notifications are dropped on read. Only `dismissible` and `personal` ones can be dismissed; a personal dismissal is stored per person on the notification itself (not in the browser), with the items it covered, and the notification returns for that person when an item joins its set.
- **Delivery.** The board refetches on the hint and on every live-update reconnect, and polls every 15 s while one is showing (60 s otherwise), because a hidden tab closes its event stream and loses hints.
- **The bar.** Critical is large and red, flashes once when it arrives and cannot be dismissed while its condition holds. Warning is amber. Info is quiet. With several, the most severe (then the oldest) shows in full and the rest as a "+N more" count that expands.
- **Main is red (critical).** Raised when the base branch's checks fail and cleared when they pass, from the same read that records `board.baseChecks`. It names the failing check and its first error, the glob whose merge turned the base red, and that globs failing the same way are waiting. It links to the failing run.
- **Board setup (readiness).** A readiness item that is **failing** (it worked and now doesn't) raises a **warning** `readiness:<item>` with the item's detail and fix, linking to the checklist (`/boards/<id>/settings#readiness`); it clears itself when the item passes. Items **missing** (never set up) raise one **info** `readiness`, "Board setup: N items to do", linking to the checklist; it is `personal`, so each person dismisses it for themselves, and it returns when a new item becomes missing. Unknown and ok items raise nothing. The server checks every board each minute and whenever the checklist is read, from the same facts the settings checklist shows. The full checklist stays in Settings (Board and repository); there is no separate readiness banner.

## Operations

Slop has two environments: a developer's laptop (Docker Compose) and production on EC2. Only production is backed up.

- **Backups:** a nightly `pg_dump` to S3 kept for 30 days by a lifecycle rule; a weekly EBS snapshot via AWS's snapshot scheduler; artifacts protected by S3 bucket versioning. One test restore after setup. Cost is well under a dollar a month.
- **Deploying slop:** CodeBuild, triggered by pushes to main, builds the image, pushes it to a registry, and updates the EC2 instance through SSM Run Command (no SSH, no stored keys). Migrations run when the app starts. CodeBuild is preferred wherever it fits.
- **Server status page** (not the System group of Settings, which lists the server's capabilities): the last \~100 errors (stored in Postgres, with time, task and message); when each automatic input was last received (GitHub and AWS webhooks, each person's Apps Script, the Slack app); the last routine run per person; the last report run; the indexing backlog. Anything silent for longer than expected shows amber.
- **Uptime:** a free external uptime checker pings the server and emails when it is down.
- **LLM spend (as built, s15f43 + s15f60):** slop multiplies each model call's token usage by a price table (`PRICE_PER_MTOK`, kept by hand, longest model-ID match; a model without a price keeps its tokens and counts as an unpriced call) and records it in the `llm_spend` ledger, one row per call, with its board, source and glob when there is one. Every board-scoped call carries that attribution as `spend` (board, source, glob) on the LLM port's request: local metadata that is never sent to the model, so it changes neither the prompt nor the tokens. The server's usage callback writes the row from Bedrock's own usage report, fire and forget: a ledger error is logged and never delays or fails the call. Sources: `kb_route`, `kb_draft`, `kb_consolidate`, `findings`, `intake`, `size_check`, `search` (chat's search rewrite), `search_index` (a merged change's "why"), `decisions`, `inbox`, `sub_limit` (the bug-reference check) and `chat` (answers, "think harder" and every turn of the code-view loop). Reviews (`review`: one row per review from the summed usage, also for an attempt that failed after calling the model; a change of only skipped files makes no call and records nothing; the cost is on the review's provenance as `costUsd`) and the knowledge page (`knowledge_page`, tokens estimated as characters / 4) record their own rows, so their calls are untagged and not counted twice. Board-less calls (the AWS sign-in probe) are only logged; Titan embeddings report no usage and aren't counted. A streamed answer the person stops, or a call that times out, reports no usage and is not counted, so the figure can run slightly low. Claude prices are Anthropic's (checked 2026-10-12): `global.` inference profiles at the base price, regional and geographic profiles (slop's `us.` defaults) with the 10% Bedrock premium; still to be checked against Bedrock's own price table. The board shows month-to-date spend, in the board's time zone, as a small line in its header ("LLM spend this month $1.23" across every source, with "+ N unpriced calls" when there are any; `GET /boards/{b}/spend`, which returns `usd`, `unpricedCalls`, `bySource` (priced cost per source) and `unpricedBySource` (unpriced calls per source)), and the line's tooltip breaks it down by source, costliest first, naming each source's unpriced calls so a source whose model lost its price still shows. An AWS Budgets email alert is the backstop. No per-user caps for now.
- **Data retention:** everything is kept by default. Any item can be deleted, and a "remove everything from this source or person" action handles departures and requests. Each deployment should check its personal-data retention obligations under the data-protection law that applies to it.

## Testing

Slop will get little human review, so automated checks act as its reviewers.

**Slop's own code**

- Strict TypeScript and linting, including the boundary rule that keeps adapters out of the core.
- Unit tests for core rules: the category/type matrix, transitions, sub-to-same conversion, label rules, ID generation, ancestry-based deployment status and time calculation. The core runs against in-memory fakes, so these are fast.
- A few Playwright smoke tests: load the board, move a glob, open an artifact.
- A short human look at the security surface even so: Cognito and Identity Center setup, IAM grants in CDK, and the GitHub App's permissions, especially its right to merge to master.

**Test results for the repos slop manages**

- Tests and snapshot or golden files are committed; run output is not.
- CodeBuild report groups hold the results (JUnit XML, LCOV or Cobertura), with raw files exported to S3 for longer retention.
- Slop reads results through the CodeBuild reports API and shows them via the glob's test icon. Branch results attach to the glob; ATF results attach to the environment's commit.
- A failure summary is posted on the PR so reviewers and Claude's auto-fix can act without AWS access.

**Local environment**

- Docker Compose runs the app and Postgres; Claude Code can start it, run tests and drive the board with Playwright.
- A dev Cognito pool with native test users provides real tokens; local Claude Code connects to the local MCP with one.
- GitHub and AWS events are tested by replaying saved real payloads; anything that creates branches or PRs uses a test GitHub App on a sandbox repo.
- Storage and Bedrock use real dev resources with a dev AWS profile. In-memory fakes are used only for core unit tests.

## Supers

Pairing work is at the developer's discretion, including up to 2 days before marking the PR ready; the aging colour applies as for any glob.

- The developer works on the glob's own branch throughout. Each push deploys it to the glob's environment (normally a branch-deploy environment the developer uses), started by slop.
- The local review runs in sessionator using the slop-delivered agents and is pushed to slop as the `local_review` artifact.
- The glob's draft PR exists from creation (a super waits in Planning and gets its branch and draft PR when a developer picks it up, row 6). It is marked ready for review through slop's `mark_ready`: in the session after `/finalise` (which `sstor --ready` sends), or with the board's Mark done (PR ready), which warns when no local review is recorded at the head. That triggers the remote review (ATF runs later, after the merge is deployed to int); results reach slop through the existing webhooks and EventBridge.
- Developers choose the glob's environment from the board's branch-deploy environments. Slop tracks no environment claims; the card shows when another glob's deploy has replaced this one in a shared environment.
- **Branches:** every glob has its own branch, always created from master. No stacking: if reviews send back serious problems, work does not continue on a new glob until they are resolved.
- `sstor --new --super` creates the glob (through `create_glob`, which returns `{ id, branch }`) and opens a session on its branch.
- **Implementation record:** sessionator updates the record from the code changes and the session conversation on each push (best effort) and always at sstor --ready and --derge, and sends it to slop. When the meeting ends and its Gemini notes arrive through the Apps Script, slop suggests attaching them to the developer's active super, a person confirms, and slop then runs one more pass merging the meeting's decisions into the record.

## Roles and permissions

Board roles are admin, dev, QA and PO.

- Roles are per board, stored on the membership record (user + board).
- Anyone can create a board; its creator is its admin. New members default to dev.
- Only admins add users and set roles; admins can do everything.
- Anyone on the board can delete globs. FR, CR and QA can only be switched on the board, not through the MCP.
- **To begin with, only admins choose sub or super** (one rule, `canChooseType` in `access.ts`, so it can be loosened in one place). Creating a glob (board form, REST, `create_glob`, inbox) as a sub or a super needs the admin role; everyone else creates sames. An explicit sub or super from a non-admin is refused with `forbidden` ("Only board admins can create a sub or a super; create it as a same and an admin can change its type"); a type nobody chose (no `type` on `create_glob`, or intake's proposal) becomes a same. Changing a glob's type to sub or super (`update_glob`, the type popover, Make it a super, undoing an escalation) needs admin too; changing to same is open to whoever can edit the glob. What slop and its agents create or change on their own behalf (split parts, hotspot refactor globs, decision globs, agent-set globs, `escalate_to_same`) is not affected. The board form and the type popover disable sub and super for non-admins with a tooltip.
- QA and PO cannot create supers (so, with the rule above, they create sames only), and they cannot start, retry, start again, resolve conflicts on, pick up or take over sames or supers (the buttons are hidden and the API and MCP refuse); developers and admins do those, and QA and PO keep them for subs.
- Users are added by email and linked to their account on first sign-in.
- **Board settings:** repo, sensitive paths, time zone, default routine owner, dev (branch-deploy) environments with their deploy projects, members and roles. There is no base-branch setting (the repo's default branch is read from GitHub), no environment role or production flag, and no default environment for subs. Wherever this document says master, it means the board's base branch.
- **Settings and Knowledge pages: what a person chooses, and what slop has learned (as built, s15f171).** The two are separate pages, and every learned value lives on exactly one.
  - *Settings* holds only what a person chooses, in six collapsible groups, each setting with a one-line plain explanation under its label (visible text, never a hover-only tooltip): **Board and repository** (name, the repository and its GitHub connection, the readiness checklist, time zone), **Members and access** (members and roles, the integration token), **Environments and deploys** (dev environments with their deploy projects; branch deploys; acceptance tests after a deploy), **Automation** (investigating big globs automatically, independent review, who may approve knowledge, effect-check window), **Reports** (the time report) and **System** (what the server can do, read-only, and for admins the chat answers rated 👎; collapsed by default and last). Each group's open state is remembered per viewer in the browser's storage (`slop.settings-group.<group>`), falling back to the default (all open but System) when storage is unavailable. Members read every setting and admins change them, with one Save for the editable groups. A link to `#readiness` opens the Board and repository group.
  - *Knowledge* has a **What slop has learned** area with one panel per topic, all collapsed by default and loading nothing until opened: **Size** (the threshold that flags a glob as big, and the forecast's calibration), **Subs** (the sub size limit), **Risk areas**, **Escalation criteria**, **Dependency rules**, **Intake** (the types intake learned from people's changes, and its accuracy), **Context tuning** and **Learning jobs**. Each panel shows the current value in plain words, the default it started from, the history of changes with their causes (collapsed, newest first) and the admin actions it has (reset to defaults, remove a learned risk area, reset a dependency rule, reset a context setting, Run now for a job). The routes and permissions are unchanged: members read, admins act. `/boards/{b}/knowledge?learned=<panel>` opens a panel; an old link `/boards/{b}/settings?section=<name>` (or `#<name>`) for a moved part redirects to its panel, and an unknown name stays on Settings.
- **Board readiness:** Settings (Board and repository) shows a checklist of what the board needs before slop can run its globs end to end: slop's GitHub App on the repo, the workflow that reports the board's gate check (`checks` by default; `.github/workflows/checks.yml`) on the base branch, with a note, from the base branch's active rules (`GET /rules/branches/{base}`), on whether that check is a required status check and whether slop's App can bypass the ruleset (the bypass list is shown only when GitHub reveals it), linking `setup-repo.sh --protect` when not, (it says nothing about a merge queue: slop merges through its own merge line. It does not read the workflow's triggers), a workflow on the base branch that runs `anthropics/claude-code-action` (what makes `@claude` comments get a response; catalog `claude.yml`, or `/install-github-app`), the agent set committed at the board's version (`.claude/slop-agent-set.json`), a build doc, dev (branch-deploy) environments, and three items slop can't check (routines for the board, the repo in the routine's repositories, the Claude GitHub App itself), which an admin ticks and which turn red when a routine failure in the last week points to them. Each item has a Fix link, and the board shows a banner while anything needs doing. Cards show a stuck hint for a ready sub with no gate check result after 15 minutes (naming the check), a passed gate that hasn't merged, or a routine failure with a known fix. What each environment is for goes in an environments KB document (catalog template).
